Deformations of the Standard Map with Prescribed Actions and Lyapunov Exponents
Yunzhe Li

Abstract
We construct nontrivial deformations of the standard map which preserve the symplectic actions, respectively the Lyapunov exponents, of infinitely many periodic orbits accumulating to an invariant curve. The proof uses a resonant normal-form construction to obtain a sequence of periodic orbits accumulating on an invariant curve with a Liouville rotation number. Within these normal forms we capture the dependence of these periodic orbits on the resonant Fourier coefficients of the dynamics on the invariant curve and, using the contraction mapping principle, obtain a suitable deformation achieving the prescribed spectral data associated with this sequence of orbits. The result can be viewed as a symplectic twist-map analogue of a length spectral nonrigidity phenomenon for Riemannian manifolds and convex billiards, and it motivates the existence problem for similar “partially length-isospectral” deformations of strictly convex billiard tables.



1
Introduction

Let
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and let
V:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function.
The
(Chirikov) standard map

\mathscr{F}
associated with the
potential

V
is the dynamical system on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R},

(1.1)

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(x+y+\dot{V}(x),\,y+\dot{V}(x)\bigr),

where
\dot{V}:=\frac{dV}{dx}
.

Suppose
(x,y)
is periodic under
\mathscr{F}
with (minimal) period
q\geq 1
and write
(x_{k},y_{k})=\mathscr{F}^{k}(x,y)
for
k=0,1,\ldots,q-1
.
Then the
symplectic action
of this periodic orbit is defined as

\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\Bigl[\frac{1}{2}\bigl(x_{k}-x_{k+1}\bigr)^{2}+V(x_{k})\Bigr]

(1.2)

with indices taken modulo
q
.
Let
\mathrm{d}_{(x,y)}\mathscr{F}^{q}

denote the differential of the iterated map
\mathscr{F}^{q}
at
(x,y)
, viewed as a linear operator on the tangent space of
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
at
(x,y)
.
The
Lyapunov exponent
of the periodic orbit
(x_{k},y_{k})_{k=0}^{q-1}
is defined to be

\det\bigl(\mathrm{d}_{(x,y)}\mathscr{F}^{q}-\id\bigr).

(1.3)

The primary goal of this paper is to construct examples of nontrivial deformations of the potential
V
that preserve the symplectic actions (respectively, the Lyapunov exponents) of infinitely many periodic orbits.
In particular, we prove:

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a nontrivial analytic family of standard maps
(\mathscr{F}_{\tau})_{\tau}
such that, for each
\tau
, the map
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
has infinitely many dynamically distinct

1
1

1

We say that two periodic orbits are
dynamically distinct
if the orbits do not coincide as sets (they are not just reparametrizations of one another, and one is not a repetition of the other).

periodic orbits depending analytically on
\tau
, and the symplectic action of each of these periodic orbits is constant in
\tau
.
The same conclusion holds with ‘symplectic action’ replaced by ‘Lyapunov exponent’.

The standard map is a prototypical example of an
exact symplectic twist map
.
It is a classical fact that for every coprime pair
(p,q)
of integers with
q>0
, a
(p,q)
-periodic orbit of an exact symplectic twist map can be given by a minimum of the
action functional

(x_{k})_{k=0}^{q-1}\mapsto\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}S(x_{k},x_{k+1}),\qquad(x_{q}=x_{0}+p),

where
S
is a generating function of the exact symplectic map.
The minimizing sequence
(x_{k})_{k=0}^{q-1}
corresponds to the first coordinate of a periodic orbit of period
q
lifted to a suitable universal cover.
Such orbits are called
minimal orbits
.

For the standard map with potential
V
, the generating function can be taken as

S(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+V(x).

(1.4)

We refer to Section
4
for more details on exact symplectic maps and the definition of
(p,q)
-periodic orbits.

Given an exact symplectic map
F
with a choice of generating function,
Mather’s beta function
is defined to be the continuous function
\beta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\beta\Big(\frac{p}{q}\Big)=\frac{1}{q}\min\{\;\text{symplectic actions of $(p,q)$-periodic orbits}\;\}.

(1.5)

For a potential
V:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
, let
\beta_{V}
denote Mather’s beta function associated with the standard map defined by the generating function (
1.4
).
We now formulate the second version of our main result.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
be such that

\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\leq q^{-70}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}

for infinitely many coprime pairs of integers
(p,q)
.
Then there exists a nontrivial real-analytic family of potentials
(V_{\tau}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R})_{\tau}
such that, for a sequence
p_{j}/q_{j}\to\omega
, the value

\beta_{V_{\tau}}\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big)

is constant in
\tau
for each
j
.

Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
are immediate consequences of Theorem
1.3
, which we now state.
We refer to Section
4
for the notation.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and let
(p_{j},q_{j})
be a sequence of coprime pairs of integers such that
p_{j}/q_{j}\to\omega
as
j\to\infty
, with
q_{j+1}>4q_{j}
and
q_{1}
sufficiently large.
Then the following statements hold.

1.

Suppose

\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|\leq q_{j}^{-70}.

(1.6)

Then there exists a real-analytic map

\alpha\mapsto\phi_{\alpha}

sending any sequence
\alpha=(\alpha_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of real numbers with
|\alpha_{j}|<1/4
to a real-analytic function
\phi_{\alpha}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\|\dddot{\phi}_{\alpha}\|\leq 1
, where
\|\;\cdot\;\|
is the Fourier-weighted norm defined in Section
4
, such that the standard map associated with a potential
V_{\alpha}
satisfying

\dot{V}_{\alpha}\bigl(t+\phi_{\alpha}(t)\bigr)\;=\;\phi_{\alpha}(t+\omega)-2\phi_{\alpha}(t)+\phi_{\alpha}(t-\omega)

(1.7)

has a sequence of
(p_{j},q_{j})
-periodic orbits
(\xi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, each depending analytically on
\alpha
, and the Lyapunov exponent of
\xi_{j}
is

-(2+\alpha_{j})\,q_{j}\Bigl(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Bigr)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}.

(1.8)

2.

Suppose

\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|\leq q_{j}^{-70}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}.

(1.9)

Then there exist
\epsilon_{0}>0
and a real-analytic map

\alpha\mapsto\phi_{\alpha}

sending any sequence
\alpha=(\alpha_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of real numbers with
|\alpha_{j}|<\epsilon_{0}
to a real-analytic function
\phi_{\alpha}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
with analytic norm
\|\dddot{\phi}_{\alpha}\|\leq 1
such that the unique standard map associated with a potential
V_{\alpha}
, which depends analytically on
\alpha
and satisfies (
1.7
),
has a sequence of
(p_{j},q_{j})
-periodic orbits
(\xi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, each depending analytically on
\alpha
, and the symplectic action of
\xi_{j}
is

\frac{q_{j}\bigl(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\bigr)^{2}}{2(1+f_{1}^{*})}\bigg[1-\frac{(2+\alpha_{j})\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}}{2\pi^{2}q_{j}^{3}}\bigg]+\bigl[\omega-(\phi_{\alpha}\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+})^{*}\bigr]\,p_{j},

(1.10)

where
\phi_{\alpha}^{+}:=\phi_{\alpha}(\cdot+\omega)
and
f_{1}^{*}
and
(\phi_{\alpha}\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+})^{*}
denote the averages over
\mathbb{T}
of the functions

f_{1}=(1+\dot{\phi}_{\alpha})^{-1}(1+\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+})^{-1}-1\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{\alpha}\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+},

respectively.
Moreover, the map
\alpha\mapsto\phi_{\alpha}
can be constructed so that the quantities
f_{1}^{*}
and
(\phi_{\alpha}\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+})^{*}
are both constant in
\alpha
and for each
j
the orbit
\xi_{j}
can be chosen as a minimal orbit, i.e., an orbit which minimizes the symplectic action among all
(p_{j},q_{j})
-orbits.

We provide some informal clarification of the statement of Theorem
1.3
.
The analyticity of the map
\alpha\mapsto\phi_{\alpha}
means the following: if
\alpha^{\tau}=(\alpha_{j}^{\tau})_{j\geq 1}
is a family of sequences parametrized by
\tau\in\mathbb{C}
such that, for each
j
, the function
\tau\mapsto\alpha_{j}^{\tau}
is analytic, then the resulting function
\phi_{\alpha^{\tau}}
depends analytically on
\tau
.
Moreover, if
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
for all
j
, then
\phi_{\alpha}
is real-valued on
\mathbb{T}
.
For complex-valued sequences
(\alpha_{j})
, analogous results hold for the complexified standard map with a complex-valued potential.

Given any real-analytic
\phi_{\alpha}
satisfying the smallness assumptions in Theorem
1.3
, one can always solve (
1.7
) for
V_{\alpha}
up to an additive constant (see Section
5
).
Therefore, there is indeed a unique standard map associated with each sequence
\alpha
via the map
\alpha\mapsto\phi_{\alpha}
.
In part (2) of Theorem
1.3
, the additive constant is fixed by the condition (
1.10
) and this constant depends analytically on
(\alpha_{j})
.

By our construction in Section
5
, each such standard map admits a rotational invariant curve (RIC) of rotation number
\omega
.
The sequence of
(p_{j},q_{j})
-periodic orbits accumulates onto this invariant curve as
j\to\infty
.
The function
\phi_{\alpha}
gives the restricted dynamics on this RIC in the sense that the map
\id+\phi_{\alpha}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
is a circle diffeomorphism that conjugates the rigid rotation
R_{\omega}
to the restriction of the dynamics to this RIC.

In our construction, the function
\phi_{\alpha}
will be chosen as a
lacunary
Fourier series in the sense that, for all
|k|\geq q_{1}
, the
k
th Fourier coefficient
\hat{\phi}_{k}
vanishes unless
k\in\pm\{q_{j},2q_{j}\}
for some
j\geq 1
.

The proof of Theorem
1.3
uses Picard iterations to construct the function
\phi_{\alpha}
as the fixed point of a certain contracting map.
Section
3
gives a more detailed outline of its proof and explains the proofs of Theorems
1.1
–
1.2
using Theorem
1.3
.
After the preparatory Sections
4
–
5
, the main technical step is the construction of resonant normal forms (Theorem
6.1
in Section
6
), which are suited to the study of
(p,q)
-periodic orbits when
p/q
approximates
\omega
sufficiently well.
The proof of the main theorem, Theorem
1.3
, is formally completed in Section
8
modulo the proof of Theorem
6.1
, which is in turn proved in the last Section
9
.

2
Motivation and background

The results in this paper are motivated by questions of
spectral rigidity
in dynamical systems.
Broadly speaking, one would like to understand to what extent a dynamical system is determined, up to an appropriate notion of conjugacy, by various spectral invariants attached to its periodic orbits.
In this paper, we focus on the
deformational
rigidity, namely, we study if a dynamical system can be nontrivially deformed without changing certain spectral invariants.

Laplace spectral rigidity and its dynamical analogues

The modern spectral rigidity problem goes back to Kac’s famous question “Can one hear the shape of a drum?”
[
10
]
.
Concretely, if two bounded domains in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
have identical Laplace spectra (with appropriate boundary conditions), must the domains be congruent?
More generally, one asks how much geometric information about a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is encoded in the spectrum of its Laplace operator.

It is now known that the answer to Kac’s question, in full generality, is negative: for instance, Gordon, Webb and Wolpert
[
6
]
, using the method of Sunada
[
23
]
, constructed isospectral nonisometric pairs of planar domains.
However, the question remains open for smooth strictly convex planar domains.

There are natural dynamical counterparts to the Laplace spectral rigidity.
In the setting of exact symplectic maps, a central object is the
action spectrum
of the map, while in the setting of geodesic flows, the analogous object is the (marked)
length spectrum
of closed geodesics.
Another dynamically relevant object for a general differentiable dynamical system is the
Lyapunov spectrum
.

More precisely, given a differentiable dynamical system
T\colon M\to M
, we define its
Lyapunov spectrum
to be the collection of Lyapunov exponents of all periodic orbits of
T
.
This can be regarded as an analogue in spirit to the length spectrum for geodesic flows.
This analogy appears explicitly in holomorphic dynamics, where McMullen
[
17
]
considers holomorphic maps
f:\Delta\to\Delta
on the unit disk and studies the quantities

\bigl|\log(f^{q})^{\prime}(z)\bigr|,

where
z
belongs to a
q
-periodic cycle of
f
.
These log-multipliers are shown to exhibit properties closely analogous to the
lengths
of closed geodesics on hyperbolic surfaces.

In the setting of expanding maps on the circle, a classical result of Shub and Sullivan
[
20
]
shows that the
marked
Lyapunov spectrum determines the map up to
smooth
conjugacy.
It is well known that every expanding map on the circle is topologically conjugate to the linear model
x\mapsto dx
for some degree
d\in\mathbb{Z}^{\times}
.
Such a conjugacy provides a natural symbolic coding of periodic orbits, and hence a canonical
marking
of the Lyapunov spectrum.
We also refer to the work of Drach and Kaloshin
[
5
]
for recent progress on an
unmarked
version of this rigidity phenomenon and for a detailed historical review of the problem therein.

Our setting is quite different from these uniformly expanding or hyperbolic examples.
The standard map is a conservative dynamical system given by an
exact symplectic twist map
defined on a cylinder, analogous to the dynamics of strictly convex billiards.
One of the goals of this paper is to exhibit a weak form of
nonrigidity
for the Lyapunov spectrum in the sense that the Lyapunov exponents from certain sequences of periodic orbits of the standard map can be preserved under nontrivial deformations.

Symplectic maps and the action spectrum

Another major aspect of this paper concerns the
action spectrum
.
Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval and
\mathscr{F}:\mathbb{T}\times I\to\mathbb{T}\times I
be an exact symplectic map with respect to the symplectic
1
-form
y\mathrm{d}x
.
With a fixed choice of generating function, each periodic orbit of
\mathscr{F}
carries a well-defined
symplectic action
obtained by evaluating the corresponding action functional over the orbit.
Explicitly, this is obtained by summing the generating function over the points of the orbit.
The
action spectrum
of
\mathscr{F}
is then the collection of symplectic actions of all periodic orbits of
\mathscr{F}
.
In the case of the standard map, the action of a periodic orbit is given explicitly by the action functional (
1.2
) introduced in Section
1
.
Likewise, each periodic orbit can be associated with the eigenvalues of its linearized Poincaré map; in our two-dimensional setting this can be conveniently encoded by the
Lyapunov exponent
(
1.3
).

Guillemin and Melrose
[
8
]
proposed to study such spectral data for exact symplectic maps and asked, in particular, whether the action spectrum (or jointly with the eigenvalues of the linearized Poincaré maps of periodic orbits) determines the map, at least locally and up to symplectic conjugation.
This is a symplectic analogue of Kac’s question.

Our main result shows that the answer is negative if one only prescribes a
subset
of the spectral data.
More precisely, Theorem
1.1
constructs a nontrivial analytic family of standard maps
(\mathscr{F}_{\tau})_{\tau\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
for which there exists an
infinite
sequence of dynamically distinct periodic orbits whose actions or Lyapunov exponents (
1.3
) are independent of
\tau
.

Length spectrum rigidity

The action spectrum is closely related to the analogous notion of the
length spectrum
of a Riemannian manifold.
Let
(M,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold.
Its
length spectrum
is the collection of lengths of closed geodesics of
(M,g)
, while the
marked length spectrum
records, for each free homotopy class, the length of the corresponding minimal closed geodesic (when it exists).
The classical length spectral rigidity problem asks whether the (marked) length spectrum determines
(M,g)
up to isometry.

There has been substantial progress in settings where the dynamics is uniformly hyperbolic.
For example, for closed surfaces of negative curvature, the works of Croke and Otal
[
3
,
19
]
show that the marked length spectrum determines the metric up to isometry.
More recently, Guillarmou and Lefeuvre
[
7
]
established
local
marked length spectral rigidity for a broad class of Anosov manifolds in any dimension.
These results provide strong evidence that, for systems with uniformly hyperbolic dynamics, the marked length spectrum is a very robust invariant.

Spectral rigidity of convex billiards

The dynamics of the billiard map associated with a strictly convex planar domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with smooth boundary provides a rather different setting from uniformly hyperbolic systems.
The billiard map is an exact symplectic twist map on a phase cylinder
\mathbb{T}\times[0,\pi]
in suitable coordinates, and its action functional coincides with (minus) the Euclidean length of the corresponding broken geodesic in
\Omega
.
Consequently, up to sign, the action spectrum of the billiard map agrees with the length spectrum of periodic billiard trajectories, and one may ask whether this spectrum determines the domain
\Omega
.
We refer to the monograph of Kozlov and Treshchëv
[
12
]
for general background on billiard dynamics.

To describe a natural marking of the billiard spectrum, we recall the notion of
rotation numbers
for twist maps.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval and
F:\mathbb{T}\times I\to\mathbb{T}\times I
be continuous.
For a periodic orbit
(x,y)
under
F
with minimal period
q
, and a lift
\widetilde{F}
of
F
to the universal cover
\mathbb{R}\times I
, there exists an integer
p\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\tilde{F}^{q}(\tilde{x},y)=(\tilde{x}+p,\,y).

Another lift
\tilde{F}^{\prime}
of
F
satisfies
\tilde{F}^{\prime}=\tilde{F}+(n,0)
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, hence

\tilde{F}^{\prime\,q}(\tilde{x},y)=(\tilde{x}+p+qn,\,y),

so
p
is determined modulo
q
by the orbit.
There is therefore a unique choice of lift
\tilde{F}
for which
p\in\{0,1,\dots,q-1\}
.
We refer to such an orbit as a
(p,q)
–periodic orbit
, or simply a
(p,q)
–orbit
of
F
.

When
F
is an exact symplectic twist map and
(p,q)
is a coprime pair, a
(p,q)
–orbit that minimizes the symplectic action among all
(p,q)
–orbits is called a
minimal orbit
, or sometimes an
Aubry–Mather orbit
, due to its central role in Aubry–Mather theory.
The number
p/q
is then called the
rotation number
of this minimal orbit.
2
2

2

However, the notion of rotation number does not apply to nonminimal periodic orbits in general: for instance, there may exist
(2p,2q)
–orbits that are not minimal.
In this paper we will therefore use the more flexible notion of
(p,q)
–orbits most of the time, without referring to rotation numbers.

In the billiard setting, one should keep in mind that a minimal orbit
maximizes
the total Euclidean length of the broken geodesic representing the periodic billiard trajectory.
The
marked length spectrum
of a billiard domain is then defined to be the map that associates to each rational number
p/q
the length of a minimal orbit with rotation number
p/q
.

Several rigidity results are known in this billiard setting.
In particular, a theorem of De Simoi, Kaloshin and Wei
[
22
]
proves
deformational
length spectral rigidity within the class of nearly circular,
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-symmetric domains.
Their proof relies on a detailed analysis of the first-order variation, under boundary deformations, of the lengths of a sequence of symmetric periodic orbits with rotation number
1/q
,
q\to\infty
, together with a delicate inversion of the resulting linearized operator.

However, the method of
[
22
]
strongly exploits the symmetry assumptions.
In particular, any deformation of the boundary which is “antisymmetric” to first order produces vanishing first-order variations of the lengths
and
Lyapunov exponents of every symmetric periodic orbit, and thus cannot be detected at the linear level.
This leaves open the possibility that there might exist nontrivial deformations which preserve the lengths of infinitely many periodic trajectories.
Our construction for the standard map can be interpreted as an explicit realization of such a phenomenon in the symplectic twist-map setting.

Rigidity by spectral data of accumulating periodic orbits

Besides the result of De Simoi, Kaloshin and Wei, we review some other circumstances where the spectral data of an accumulating sequence of periodic orbits is successfully exploited to yield rigidity results.

In the work of Huang, Kaloshin and Sorrentino
[
9
]
, they show that, for a generic billiard domain, the eigendata of minimal orbits can be recovered from the marked length spectrum.
In fact, one recovers the Lyapunov exponent of a minimal orbit of rotation number
p/q
using only the lengths of minimal orbits whose rotation numbers converge to
p/q
.

We also mention work of De Simoi, Kaloshin and Leguil
[
4
]
, which considers dispersing billiard systems consisting of three scatterers with axial symmetry and shows that the marked length spectrum determines the geometry of the scatterers.
Here, the marking is given by the symbolic sequence of scatterers hit by the orbit over one period.
In that work, the authors consider the lengths of a sequence of periodic orbits converging to a homoclinic orbit of a particular
2
-periodic orbit.
Using the spectral data from this sequence, one reconstructs the Taylor series of the boundaries of the scatterers at the end-points of the
2
-periodic orbit.
This result has been extended by Osterman
[
18
]
, who considers the same setting without symmetry assumptions and shows that the marked length spectrum determines the analytic conjugacy class of the billiard map.

Notably, these positive results mentioned above make use not of the
full
spectrum, but only of a countable subset corresponding to a sequence of orbits accumulating on some invariant “boundary” or “axis of symmetry” of the dynamics.
On the other hand, our main result shows that, in the standard-map setting, spectral data associated with certain accumulating sequences of periodic orbits are
not
deformationally rigid.
This motivates the following subtle question about which countable subsets of the spectrum suffice to determine the underlying dynamics.

Question 1
.

Does there exist a nonisometric deformation of a smooth strictly convex planar billiard domain which preserves the lengths of infinitely many periodic orbits accumulating to the boundary, to a periodic orbit, or to an invariant curve?

There is a positive answer to a
nondeformative
version of Question
1
: Buhovsky and Kaloshin
[
2
]
constructed a pair of billiard domains such that the marked length spectra of the two domains coincide along a sequence of rotation numbers
1/q_{j}
with
q_{j}\to\infty
.

The methods in this current paper do not answer Question
1
.
The key issue is that the proof of our main theorem, Theorem
1.3
, relies crucially on the special structure of the standard map: one can explicitly construct potentials for a standard map admitting an invariant curve with a prescribed dynamics restricted on that curve.
In the setting of billiards, the standard tool for obtaining invariant curves is KAM theory, but the Liouville-type arithmetic condition (
1.9
) required in our construction is incompatible with the usual Brjuno-type conditions needed for KAM theory.

Mather’s beta function

Another spectral invariant often considered is Mather’s beta function and its infinite jet at particular points.
Given a general exact symplectic twist map and a choice of generating function, Mather’s beta function
\beta
is defined, as in (
1.5
), to be a continuous function whose value at a rational number
p/q
is
1/q
times the action of the minimal orbits of rotation number
p/q
.
It is known that
\beta
is convex on
\mathbb{Q}
and therefore extends continuously, by density, to an interval of
\mathbb{R}
called the
rotation interval
of the map.

If Mather’s beta function admits an asymptotic expansion at
\omega
of the form

\beta(\alpha)\sim\sum_{k\geq 0}c_{k}(\alpha-\omega)^{k},

(2.1)

we call the sequence
(c_{k})
the
infinite jet
of
\beta
at
\omega
, by analogy with the jets of differentiable functions.
More precisely, the asymptotics (
2.1
) means that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any sequence
\alpha_{j}\to\omega
,

\frac{1}{(\alpha_{j}-\omega)^{N}}\bigg(\beta(\alpha_{j})-\sum_{k=0}^{N}c_{k}(\alpha_{j}-\omega)^{k}\bigg)\to 0\quad\text{as }j\to\infty.

We refer to
[
21
,
15
]
for more details on Mather’s beta function.

In the billiard setting, Mather’s beta function admits an infinite jet at
0
, which is commonly known as the Marvizi–Melrose invariants
[
14
]
.
Hence, if a deformation of a billiard domain preserves a sequence of orbits accumulating to the boundary, then this deformation preserves all Marvizi–Melrose invariants.
In particular, Buhovsky and Kaloshin
[
2
]
used this observation to construct nonisometric pairs of domains with identical Marvizi–Melrose invariants.

We recall that a
rotational invariant curve (RIC)
of a map
F:\mathbb{T}\times I\to\mathbb{T}\times I
is the graph

C=\{(x,h(x)):\ x\in\mathbb{T}\},

of a continuous function
h:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
, such that
C
is invariant under
F
.
Using Birkhoff normal forms, one can show that if the symplectic map is sufficiently smooth and it has an RIC whose restricted dynamics conjugates to a rigid rotation
x\mapsto x+\omega
by a
Diophantine
number (i.e.
\omega
is irrational and
\sup_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}|q\omega-p|^{-1}
grows at most polynomially in
q
), then Mather’s beta function admits an infinite jet at
\omega
.
Thus, if a deformation of the system preserves a sequence of orbits accumulating to such an invariant curve, then this deformation preserves the infinite jet of Mather’s beta function at
\omega
.

This leads to the following question, which is in some sense weaker than Question
1
.

Question 2
.

Does there exist a nonisometric deformation of a smooth strictly convex planar billiard domain which preserves the infinite jet of Mather’s beta function (when it exists) at some
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
?

We remark that Mather
[
16
]
proved that Mather’s beta function is differentiable at every
irrational

\omega
and generically fails to be differentiable at rational
\omega
.
In fact, Mather’s beta function is differentiable at
p/q
if and only if there exists an RIC conjugate to a rigid rotation by
p/q
.
However, it is not clear in general whether Mather’s beta function has an infinite jet at a frequency
\omega
that is irrational but not Diophantine.
Hence, our result Theorem
1.2
does not provide information relevant to Question
2
, due to the strong arithmetic condition (
1.9
).
This motivates a further regularity question for Mather’s beta function.

Question 3
.

Under what conditions does Mather’s beta function admit an infinite jet at a given
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
?

3
An outline of the proof

We first explain how Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
follow from Theorem
1.3
.

To obtain Theorem
1.1
from Theorem
1.3
, we consider a one-parameter analytic family
\alpha^{\tau}=(\alpha_{j}^{\tau})_{j\geq 1}
of sequences such that
\alpha_{j}^{\tau}
is constant in
\tau
for all
j\geq 2
, and only the first parameter
\alpha_{1}^{\tau}
varies.
Applying Theorem
1.3
to this family with a choice of
\phi_{\alpha}
keeping the averaged quantities
f_{1}^{*}
and
(\phi_{\alpha}\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+})^{*}
constant in
\alpha
, we deduce from the explicit formula (
1.10
) that the action (or Lyapunov exponent) of each
(p_{j},q_{j})
–orbit with
j\geq 2
is constant in
\tau
.
Since the pairs
(p_{j},q_{j})
are coprime and the
q_{j}
are strictly increasing, these periodic orbits are dynamically distinct, yielding Theorem
1.1
.

Finally, Theorem
1.2
follows by combining Theorem
1.3
with the definition of Mather’s beta function.
Indeed, by Theorem
1.3
, the sequence of
(p_{j},q_{j})
-orbits can be chosen as minimal orbits. Hence, they control the value of Mather’s beta function at
p_{j}/q_{j}
. For a suitable choice of the family
\alpha^{\tau}
as above (again varying only
\alpha_{1}^{\tau}
), the values of
\beta_{V_{\tau}}(p_{j}/q_{j})
remain constant in
\tau
for the whole subsequence
p_{j}/q_{j}\to\omega
, which is precisely the statement of Theorem
1.2
.

We now outline the proof of the main result, Theorem
1.3
.

Step 1. Parameterization by a rotational invariant curve.

The starting point is the elementary observation that
a rotational invariant curve (RIC) with rotation number
\omega
determines the standard map
.
More precisely, suppose there exist a circle diffeomorphism
u:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
and a smooth map
v:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\mathscr{F}(u(t),v(t))=(u(t+\omega),\,v(t+\omega))

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.
Writing
u=\id+\phi
with
\phi:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
, and expressing the standard map in the coordinates
(u,v)
, one obtains an explicit relation

\dot{V}\bigl(t+\phi(t)\bigr)\;=\;\phi(t+\omega)-2\phi(t)+\phi(t-\omega)

(3.1)

between the potential
V
and the function
\phi
describing the RIC (see Section
5
for details).
In particular, once
\phi
and
\omega
are fixed, the potential
V
is determined by (
3.1
) up to an additive constant.

Thus, for fixed
\omega
, the class of standard maps admitting an RIC of rotation number
\omega
and whose dynamics on the RIC is conjugate to the rigid rotation
R_{\omega}
is parametrized by the function
\phi:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
.
In the proof of Theorem
1.3
we work inside this class: we first construct a family of functions
\phi_{\alpha}
, and then define the corresponding potentials
V_{\alpha}
by solving (
3.1
), choosing the additive constant appropriately.
To facilitate computation, we first consider coordinates adapted to this RIC, where the standard map writes

\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x+\omega+y+f(x,y)\\
y+g(x,y)\end{pmatrix}

(3.2)

where the RIC corresponds to
\{y=0\}
and the functions
f
and
g
depend implicitly on
(\omega,\phi)
.

Step 2. The heuristic and a
q
–resonant normal form near the RIC

Fix a suitable
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
.
We choose a sequence of coprime integer pairs
(p_{j},q_{j})
with
q_{j}>0
and
p_{j}/q_{j}\to\omega
as
j\to\infty
, subject to the arithmetic condition (
1.6
) in Theorem
1.3
.

The guiding heuristic is that, for each such rational approximation
p_{j}/q_{j}
, the
q_{j}
-th Fourier mode of
\phi
should dominate the spectral data of
(p_{j},q_{j})
–periodic orbits
when
p_{j}/q_{j}
approximates
\omega
sufficiently well.
Here, by
spectral data
we mean either the symplectic actions or the Lyapunov exponents of these periodic orbits.
The idea is that if
q_{j}
and
q_{k}
are very far apart, then the contribution of the
q_{k}
–th Fourier mode to the dynamics of
(p_{j},q_{j})
–orbits should be averaged out because the corresponding periods are incompatible.
This viewpoint is inspired by resonant averaging techniques in Hamiltonian dynamics (see, for example,
[
1
]
).
The heuristic above is made precise by a resonant normal-form construction described in Section
6
.
Adapting the method of
[
13
]
to the standard map, we show that, in a complex neighbourhood of the line
\{y=p/q-\omega\}
, the standard map (
3.2
) can be conjugated to a
q
–resonant normal form
.
More concretely, in these
q
-dependent coordinates the map is conjugate to a map (defined after re-centering
y
-coordinate at
p/q-\omega
) of the form

\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x+p/q+y\\
y+g_{q}(x,y)\end{pmatrix},

(3.3)

where
g_{q}(x,0)
is
almost

1/q
–periodic in
x
.

We consider the (dominant)
resonant part

\langle g_{q}\rangle_{q}^{N}
of
g_{q}
, obtained by truncating its Fourier series to modes of order at most
N
that are multiples of
q
.
Theorem
6.1
shows that, for
N
not too large and
|\omega-p/q|
small enough, the resonant part
\langle g_{q}\rangle_{q}^{N}
can be well-approximated by the resonant part of an explicit expression in terms of the leading Taylor expansion
f_{1}(x)
of
f(x,y)
at
y=0
, which in turn depends rather explicitly on
\phi
.
By further analysis in Section
7
, we formalize the heuristic in Lemma
7.2
: the dynamics governing the
(p,q)
–periodic orbits depend mainly on the Fourier coefficients of
\phi
at orders
kq
and only weakly on the remaining coefficients.

Step 3. A fixed-point argument for the resonant data.

In Section
7
we use a Picard iteration scheme to construct
\phi_{\alpha}
so that the resonant normal forms
g_{q_{j}}
associated with the approximants
(p_{j},q_{j})
have a prescribed structure.

Using the estimates from Theorem
6.1
, we show that for each
q
the map
\phi\mapsto\langle\partial_{x}g_{q}\rangle_{q}^{N}
is close, in a suitable analytic norm and with appropriate scaling, to the map
\phi\mapsto\langle\dddot{\phi}\rangle_{q}^{N}
(the resonant part of the third derivative of
\phi
), provided
q
is large.
Because the periods
q_{j}
grow sufficiently fast, the influence of the
q_{k}
–th resonant block on the
q_{j}
–th resonant block is very small when
k\neq j
.
This near-decoupling allows us to prescribe the resonant parts
\langle g_{q_{j}}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}

simultaneously
for the infinite sequence
(p_{j},q_{j})
by solving a fixed-point equation for
\phi
.

This fixed-point problem is encoded in a nonlinear operator on a Banach space of analytic functions, and we prove that this operator is a contraction.
The resulting contraction mapping theorem is formulated as Theorem
7.1
and yields an
inversion
of the map

\phi\mapsto\Big(\langle\partial_{x}g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\Big)_{j\geq 1}.

(3.4)

In other words, Theorem
7.1
allows us to control the resonant parts of the
q_{j}
–resonant normal forms
g_{q_{j}}
essentially independently by tuning
\phi
.

Step 4. Construction of periodic orbits and computation of spectral data.

In Section
8
we apply the general fixed-point scheme proved in Section
7
to construct and analyse
(p_{j},q_{j})
–periodic orbits explicitly.
For a sequence of the parameters
\sigma_{j}
, we apply the inverse of (
3.4
) to the choice
N_{j}=2q_{j}
and

\langle\partial_{x}g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\;=\;\sigma_{j}\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\bigl(p_{j}/q_{j}-\omega\bigr)^{2}\cos(2\pi q_{j}x).

Using
g_{q_{j}}\approx\langle g_{q_{j}}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
, one can construct a
(p_{j},q_{j})
–periodic orbit that closely shadows the
equidistributed
orbits
(kp_{j}/q_{j},0)_{k=0,\ldots,q_{j}-1}
.

By analysing directly the Jacobian matrix of the normal form (
3.3
) along this periodic orbit, we obtain an explicit expression for the Lyapunov exponent in terms of the parameter
\sigma_{j}
.
Furthermore, using the near-equidistribution property of the orbit together with the structure of the normal form, we derive a corresponding estimate for the symplectic action of the orbit, again expressed in terms of
\sigma_{j}
.
In the final step, we perform a second Picard-type iteration, this time in a suitable Banach of sequences
(\sigma_{j})
, to adjust the parameters
\sigma_{j}
so that the Lyapunov exponent (or, in the second part of Theorem
1.3
, the action) of the
j
–th periodic orbit attains the prescribed value.

4
Notations and basic definitions

For
b>0
, let
\mathbb{D}_{b}
denote the open disc in
\mathbb{C}
of radius
b
centred at the origin.
We write
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
for the real
1
-torus. For
a>0
, define the complex neighbourhood

\mathbb{T}_{a}:=\{\,z\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}:|\Im z|<a\,\}.

If
x\in\mathbb{T}_{a}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we write
x+z
for the class in
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
obtained by adding representatives of
x
and
z
. In particular, adding a
real
number preserves
\mathbb{T}_{a}
.

For
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, the
rigid rotation by
\omega
is

\displaystyle R_{\omega}:\mathbb{T}_{a}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{a},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto x+\omega.

For a function
u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}
, we shall denote by

u^{\pm}:=u\circ R_{\pm\omega}

the translations of
u
by an
\pm\omega
-rotation.

We write
A\lesssim B
to mean that
A\leq c\,B
for some universal constant
c>0
(independent of any parameters involved in
A
and
B
).

For a function
u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}
, we denote by
\dot{u}
,
\ddot{u}
, and
\dddot{u}
its first, second, and third derivatives with respect to
x
.

Analytic norms.

Let
u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic. Its supremum norm on
\mathbb{T}_{a}
is

|u|_{a}:=\sup\{\,|u(x)|:x\in\mathbb{T}_{a}\,\}.

Expanding
u
into its Fourier series

u(x)\;=\;\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{u}_{k}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx},\qquad\widehat{u}_{k}\;=\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}kx}\,\mathrm{d}x,

(where
\mathrm{d}x
denotes the normalized Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}
), we define the
Fourier-weighted norm

\|u\|_{a}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|\widehat{u}_{k}|\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi a|k|}.

The spaces

\{u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}:|u|_{a}<\infty\}\quad\text{and}\quad\{u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}:\|u\|_{a}<\infty\}

are Banach spaces. Moreover, the
|\cdot|_{a}
-space is a Banach algebra, i.e.
|fg|_{a}\leq|f|_{a}\,|g|_{a}
, and (see Lemma
C.1
in the Appendix) the
\|\cdot\|_{a}
-space is also a Banach algebra.
We also introduce

\|u\|_{a}^{\prime}:=\|\dddot{u}\|_{a}.

Since we often take
a=1
, we suppress the subscript in this case and write simply

|u|:=|u|_{1},\qquad\|u\|:=\|u\|_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|u\|^{\prime}:=\|u\|_{1}^{\prime}.

For
f:\mathbb{T}_{a}\times\mathbb{D}_{b}\to\mathbb{C}
analytic, set

|f|_{a,b}:=\sup\{\,|f(x,y)|:x\in\mathbb{T}_{a},\ y\in\mathbb{D}_{b}\,\}.

Resonant projections in Fourier space.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic. We write
\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{q}
for the projection of
u
onto the
q\mathbb{Z}
-modes (the
1/q
-periodization), and
\left\{{u}\right\}_{q}:=u-\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{q}
for the complementary part:

\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{q}(x)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
q\mid k\end{subarray}}\widehat{u}_{k}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx}\;=\;\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}u\Bigl(x+\frac{j}{q}\Bigr),

\left\{{u}\right\}_{q}(x)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
q\nmid k\end{subarray}}\widehat{u}_{k}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx}.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define the low/high-frequency truncations

\left\langle{u}\right\rangle^{N}(x):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|k|\leq N\end{subarray}}\widehat{u}_{k}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx},\qquad\left\langle{u}\right\rangle^{>N}(x):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|k|>N\end{subarray}}\widehat{u}_{k}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx},

and combine them as

\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{q}^{N}(x):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
q\mid k,\ |k|\leq N\end{subarray}}\widehat{u}_{k}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx}.

We also use

u^{*}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\qquad u^{\bullet}:=u-u^{*},

for the average and zero-average parts of
u
.

For
f:\mathbb{T}_{a}\times\mathbb{D}_{b}\to\mathbb{C}
analytic and fixed
y\in\mathbb{D}_{b}
, let
\widehat{f}(y)_{k}
be the
k
th Fourier coefficient of
x\mapsto f(x,y)
:

\widehat{f}(y)_{k}\;=\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x,y)\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}kx}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We apply the same resonant projection notations
\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{q}
,
\left\langle{f}\right\rangle^{N}
,
f^{*}
,
f^{\bullet}
etc., in the
x
-variable (with
y
fixed), e.g.

\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{q}^{N}(x,y)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
q\mid k,\ |k|\leq N\end{subarray}}\widehat{f}(y)_{k}\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx};\qquad f^{*}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x,y)\mathrm{d}x.

Exact symplectic maps and actions.

We recall that a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
map
\mathscr{F}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
is said to be
exact symplectic
if the differential
1
-form
\mathscr{F}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-y\mathrm{d}x
is exact, i.e.

\mathscr{F}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-y\mathrm{d}x=\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}

(4.1)

for some function
\mathscr{S}
on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
.
The function
\mathscr{S}
, which is unique up to an additive constant, is called a
generating function
of
\mathscr{F}
.
In particular, taking the exterior derivative of (
4.1
) shows that
\mathscr{F}
preserves the canonical area form
\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y
.
Let a generating function
\mathscr{S}
be fixed.
Let
(\mathscr{F}^{k}(x_{0},y_{0}))_{k=0}^{q-1}
be a
q
-periodic orbit of
\mathscr{F}
.
Then the
(symplectic) action
of this orbit is defined as

\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\mathscr{S}(\mathscr{F}^{k}(x_{0},y_{0})).

Denote
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\mathscr{F}(x,y)
.
If
x^{\prime}
is a monotone function in
x
for each fixed
y
, then
\mathscr{F}
is called a
twist map
and, in this case, the lift of the map
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(x,y)\mapsto(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
is a diffeomorphism
\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Hence, one can lift
\mathscr{S}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and then consider
\mathscr{S}
as a function in the
(x,x^{\prime})
-variables.
The
(x,x^{\prime})
-coordinates are sometimes called the
Lagrangian coordinates
.
If
\mathscr{F}
is the standard map associated with a potential
V(x)
, then its generating function can be given by

\mathscr{S}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+V(x).

(4.2)

We recall that the notion of
(p,q)
-periodic orbits and minimal orbits have been defined in Section
2
.
Given a coprime pair
(p,q)
, Birkhoff proved that there exists at least two dynamically distinct
(p,q)
-orbits.
Besides a minimal orbit with rotation number
p/q
, another
(p,q)
-periodic orbit can be obtained using a ‘mountain pass’ argument (see for e.g.,
[
11
, Ch. 9]
).
It is customary to call this additional
(p,q)
-periodic orbit a
minimax
orbit.

5
Invariant curves and adapted coordinates

Recovering the potential from an RIC.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be the standard map (
1.1
) associated with a potential
V
.
A special feature of (
1.1
) is that it is uniquely determined by any one of its rotational invariant curves that conjugate to rigid rotations (if it has any).
We explain this now.

Fix
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose
\mathscr{F}
has a rotational invariant curve (RIC) in the sense defined in Section
2
and assume furthermore that the dynamics on
C
is conjugate to the rigid rotation
R_{\omega}:x\mapsto x+\omega
.
Concretely, there exists a circle diffeomorphism
u:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
and a map
v:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
such that the map
t\mapsto(u(t),v(t))
parametrizes
C
and

\mathscr{F}(u(t),v(t))\;=\;\bigl(u(t+\omega),\,v(t+\omega)\bigr)\quad\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{T}.

(5.1)

Write
u(t)=t+\phi(t)
with a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function
\phi:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
; then
u
is an orientation preserving circle diffeomorphism iff
\dot{\phi}>-1
.
By choosing a lift, we consider
u
and
v
as functions on
\mathbb{R}
with
u(t+1)=u(t)+1
and
v(t+1)=v(t)
, and interpret (
5.1
) using lifts so that equalities are in
\mathbb{R}
for the
x
-component. From (
1.1
) and (
5.1
),

v(t+\omega)\;=\;u(t+\omega)-u(t),\qquad\dot{V}\bigl(u(t)\bigr)\;=\;v(t+\omega)-v(t).

(5.2)

Substituting
u=\id+\phi
and eliminating
v
, we obtain

\dot{V}\bigl(t+\phi(t)\bigr)\;=\;\phi(t+\omega)-2\phi(t)+\phi(t-\omega)\,.

(5.3)

Thus, the pair
(\omega,\phi)
determines
\dot{V}
on
\mathbb{T}
, and hence determines
V
up to an additive constant.

Existence of
V
for a given conjugacy.

Let
u=\id+\phi
with
\dot{\phi}>-1
and set
\phi^{\pm}:=\phi\circ R_{\pm\omega}
as in Section
4
. Define

w(x)\;:=\;\bigl(\phi^{+}-2\phi+\phi^{-}\bigr)\circ u^{-1}(x).

We claim
\int_{\mathbb{T}}w(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0
, which implies that there exists
V:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
(unique up to an additive constant) such that
\dot{V}=w
and hence (
5.3
) holds.

Indeed, using the change of variables
x=u(t)
and that
\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\phi^{+}-2\phi+\phi^{-})\,\mathrm{d}t=0
and
\int_{\mathbb{T}}2\phi(t)\dot{\phi}(t)\mathrm{d}t=0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}w(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigl(\phi^{+}(t)-2\phi(t)+\phi^{-}(t)\bigr)\,(1+\dot{\phi}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigl(\phi^{+}(t)-2\phi(t)+\phi^{-}(t)\bigr)\,\dot{\phi}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi(t+\omega)\,\dot{\phi}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\;+\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi(t-\omega)\,\dot{\phi}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\dot{\phi}(t+\omega)\,\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\;+\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi(t)\,\dot{\phi}(t+\omega)\,\mathrm{d}t\;=\;0,

where in the last line we used an integration by parts on the first term.

We summarize:

Lemma 5.1
.

For any
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\phi:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\dot{\phi}>-1
, there exists a potential
V
(unique up to an additive constant) such that the standard map associated with
V
admits an RIC whose restricted dynamics conjugates to
R_{\omega}
by the diffeomorphism
u=\id+\phi
.

Standard maps in adapted coordinates.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be the unique standard map having an RIC whose restricted dynamics conjugates to
R_{\omega}
by
\id+\phi
as in Lemma
5.1
.

To facilitate later calculations, we introduce coordinates on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
adapted to this RIC.
We parametrize the RIC by
t\mapsto(u(t),v(t))
as in (
5.1
).
Consider the area-preserving change of coordinates
\mathscr{H}
that ‘straightens’ the invariant curve,

\mathscr{H}(x,y)=\Big(u(x),v(x)+\frac{y}{1+\dot{\phi}(x)}\Big).

(5.4)

Hence
\mathscr{F}\circ\mathscr{H}(x,0)=\mathscr{H}(x+\omega,0)
.
Define
F:=\mathscr{H}^{-1}\circ\mathscr{F}\circ\mathscr{H}
and functions
f,g:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
so that

F(x,y)=(x+\omega+y+f(x,y),\ y+g(x,y)).

(5.5)

A straightforward substitution of
v(\cdot+\omega)=u(\cdot+\omega)-u(\cdot)
and
u=\id+\phi
yields a set of equations characterizing
f
and
g
:

\begin{cases}y+f(x,y)=\frac{y}{1+\dot{\phi}(x)}+\phi(x+\omega)-\phi(x+\omega+y+f(x,y))\\
y+g(x,y)=\big(y+f(x,y)+\phi(x+y+f(x,y))-\phi(x)\big)\big(1+\dot{\phi}(x+\omega+y+f(x,y))\big).\end{cases}

(5.6)

Setting
y=0
in (
5.6
) gives
f(x,0)=g(x,0)=0
, as expected since
F(x,0)=(x+\omega,0)
.

In the sequel, we will call
F
the
standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in the adapted coordinates
.

Actions of periodic orbits

Next, we consider the generating function of the standard map
F=\mathscr{H}^{-1}\circ\mathscr{F}\circ\mathscr{H}
associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in the adapted coordinates as defined in the preceding paragraph.
Since
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{F}
are area-preserving, the map
F
is area preserving.
Since
F
preserves
\mathbb{T}\times\{0\}
, the integral of the differential 1-form
F^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-y\mathrm{d}x
over the circle
\mathbb{T}\times\{0\}
vanishes.
Thus
F^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-y\mathrm{d}x
belongs to the zero cohomology class by Poincaré duality, and is therefore exact.
It follows that
F
is an exact symplectic map with respect to the symplectic 1-form
y\mathrm{d}x
.
Let us now find a generating function of
F
in terms of
\phi
.

Note that the differential
1
-form
\mathscr{H}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-y\mathrm{d}x
is closed by area preservation.
Since the de Rham cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
is generated by the class of
\mathrm{d}x
where
x
parametrizes
\mathbb{T}
, we can write

\mathscr{H}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)=y\mathrm{d}x+\lambda\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}\Sigma

(5.7)

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Sigma:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mathscr{S}
be a generating function of the standard map
\mathscr{F}
.
Using
\mathscr{F}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-y\mathrm{d}x=\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}
and (
5.7
), we compute:

\displaystyle F^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)=

\displaystyle F^{*}(\mathscr{H}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-(\lambda\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}\Sigma))

(5.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-F^{*}(\lambda\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}\Sigma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)+\mathscr{H}^{*}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S})-F^{*}(\lambda\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}\Sigma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y\mathrm{d}x+\lambda\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}\Sigma+\mathscr{H}^{*}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S})-F^{*}(\lambda\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{d}\Sigma)

whereas
\lambda
can be computed by integrating
\mathscr{H}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)
over the circle
\mathbb{T}\times\{0\}
and using (
5.4
), (
5.2
) and the relation
u=\id+\phi
:

\displaystyle\lambda=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\lambda\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\{0\}}\mathscr{H}^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}v(x)(1+\dot{\phi}(x))\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\omega+\phi(x)-\phi(x-\omega))(1+\dot{\phi}(x))\mathrm{d}x=\omega-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi(x-\omega)\dot{\phi}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\omega-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi\dot{\phi}^{+}.

Substituting into (
5.8
), we obtain a generating function
S
of
F
:

S=\mathscr{S}\circ\mathscr{H}+\bigg(\omega-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi\dot{\phi}^{+}\bigg)(x-x\circ F)+(\Sigma-\Sigma\circ F)

(5.9)

where
x-x\circ F
is interpreted using a lift of
F
. By (
5.5
), we have simply
x-x\circ F=-(\omega+y+f(x,y))
.
Suppose
(F^{k}(x_{0},y_{0}))_{k=0}^{q-1}
is a
(p,q)
-periodic orbit of
F
.
Then the sum of
x-x\circ F
over this orbit is exactly
-p
by the definition of
(p,q)
-periodic orbits (see Section
4
), and the sum of the third term in (
5.9
) vanishes by a telescopic sum.
Hence, the symplectic action of this orbit with respect to the generating function (
5.9
) writes

\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}S\circ F^{k}(x_{0},y_{0})=-\bigg(\omega-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi\dot{\phi}^{+}\bigg)p+\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\mathscr{S}\circ\mathscr{H}\circ F^{k}(x_{0},y_{0}).

(5.10)

Observe that the term
\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\mathscr{S}\circ\mathscr{H}(F^{k}(x_{0},y_{0}))
is the symplectic action of the corresponding periodic orbit under the original standard map
\mathscr{F}
, with a generating function
\mathscr{S}
in the form of (
4.2
) for a potential
V
satisfying (
5.3
).
Looking forward, we will actually evaluate via (
5.10
) the
\mathscr{S}
-action
\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\mathscr{S}\circ\mathscr{H}(F^{k}(x_{0},y_{0}))
in terms of the
S
-action
\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}S(F^{k}(x_{0},y_{0}))
associated with the map
F
in adapted coordinates.
To this end, we now derive a separate formula for
S
.

By taking the derivative of the first line of (
5.6
) in
y
, it is easy to see that
\partial f/\partial y>-1
as long as
\dot{\phi}>-1
over
\mathbb{T}
.
Therefore, the map
F
is a twist map.
After lifting
S
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and transform to the Lagrangian coordinates
(x,x^{\prime})
, we deduce from
F^{*}(y\mathrm{d}x)-y\mathrm{d}x=\mathrm{d}S
that, in a neighbourhood of
\{y=0\}
,

F(x,y)=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\frac{\partial S}{\partial x}(x,x^{\prime})=-y\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial S}{\partial x^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}.

(5.11)

Using (
5.11
), and regarding
x^{\prime}(x,y)
as a function in terms of
(x,y)
, we can calculate the derivatives of
S(x,x^{\prime}(x,y))
in
x
and
y
as follows.

\frac{\partial S}{\partial x}(x,y)=\frac{\partial S}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial S}{\partial x^{\prime}}\frac{\partial x^{\prime}}{\partial x}=y^{\prime}\frac{\partial x^{\prime}}{\partial x}-y\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial S}{\partial y}(x,y)=\frac{\partial S}{\partial x^{\prime}}\frac{\partial x^{\prime}}{\partial y}=y^{\prime}\frac{\partial x^{\prime}}{\partial y}.

Substituting
x^{\prime}=x+\omega+y+f(x,y)
and
y^{\prime}=y+g(x,y)
, we then obtain explicitly

\frac{\partial S}{\partial x}(x,y)=(y+g(x,y))\Big(1+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(x,y)\Big)-y;\qquad\frac{\partial S}{\partial y}(x,y)=(y+g(x,y))\Big(1+\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}(x,y)\Big).

(5.12)

Hence, the
F
-generating function
S
can be obtained by integrating the second equation of (
5.12
) in
y
.

In particular, since
g(x,0)=0
, we see from the first equation in (
5.12
) that
S
is constant along
\{y=0\}
.
By adjusting the additive constant of the potential
V
solving (
5.3
), we may adjust
\mathscr{S}
(and hence
S
) by an additive constant so that (
5.9
) vanishes identically on the RIC.
This choice fixes
\mathscr{S}
and
S
for a given pair
(\omega,\phi)
; we call
\mathscr{S}
the
normalized generating function
associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in the original coordinates, and
S
the corresponding normalized generating function in the adapted coordinates.

6
Resonant coordinates

The main result of this section is Theorem
6.1
, where we obtain a ‘
q
–resonant normal form’ for the standard map
F
associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in the adapted coordinates constructed in Section
5
.
The normal form is valid when
\omega
is sufficiently well approximated by a rational number
p/q
with large denominator
q
.

The purpose of this normal form is to capture the dynamics of
F
in a small neighbourhood of the circle

\mathbb{T}\times\{p/q-\omega\}

and to show that, roughly speaking, the dynamics there depends strongly on the resonant Fourier modes of
\phi
, i.e. on the Fourier coefficients
\hat{\phi}_{k}
with
q\mid k
, and is only weakly influenced by the nonresonant modes.
Localization near
\mathbb{T}\times\{p/q-\omega\}
is important because the dynamics there is close to a rigid rotation in
x
by the rational angle
p/q
and, at least when
\phi
is small, all
(p,q)
–periodic orbits of
F
are expected to lie nearby.

The following theorem is the technical core of the paper.
For technical reasons, we consider the complexified standard maps in this theorem: we can obviously define an analytic map
\mathscr{F}
using the formula (
1.1
) with a complex analytic potential
V
, which can in turn be given by a real
\omega
and a complex analytic function
\phi:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
using the construction of Section
5
.
By abuse of notation we will still call the resulting map
F
the standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in adapted coordinates, even though one may not be able to iterate
F
on the real cylinder
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
indefinitely unless
\phi
is real-valued.
The formulae (
5.6
) describing the map
F
still hold, of course.
The corresponding normalized generating function
S
will be in general complex valued and the ‘symplectic actions’ obtained by summing over periodic orbits are complex quantities.
We refer to Section
4
for the notation.

Theorem 6.1
.

Fix
\nu_{0}:=64
,
\nu\geq\nu_{0}+6
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and let
\phi:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic, of zero average, with
\|\dddot{\phi}\|\leq 1
.
Let
F
be the standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in adapted coordinates.
Suppose
q
is sufficiently large and

\Big|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big|\leq q^{-\nu}\qquad\text{for some }p\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.1)

Then there exists an analytic change of coordinates

H_{q}:\mathbb{T}_{a}\times\mathbb{D}_{b}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{1}\times\mathbb{C},\qquad a=1-\frac{3}{q},\quad b=\frac{1}{5}\Big|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big|,

with the following properties.

1.

The map
H_{q}
is close to the translation by
p/q-\omega
in
y
in the sense that

H_{q}:(x,y)\longmapsto\bigl(x+h_{q,1}(x,y),\,y+p/q-\omega+h_{q,2}(x,y)\bigr)

(6.2)

where

|h_{q,1}|_{a,b}\lesssim q\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;\quad|h_{q,2}|_{a,b}<\frac{1}{10}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|.

(6.3)

2.

Defining
F_{q}=H_{q}^{-1}\circ F\circ H_{q}
, we have

F_{q}:\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}x+p/q+y\\
y+g_{q}(x,y)\end{pmatrix}

(6.4)

for some analytic
g_{q}:\mathbb{T}_{a}\times\mathbb{D}_{b}\to\mathbb{C}
such that, with
N:=\kappa q
,

\displaystyle|g_{q}|_{a,b}\;\lesssim\;\Big|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big|^{2},

(6.5)

\displaystyle\bigl|g_{q}-\langle g_{q}\rangle_{q}^{N}\bigr|_{1/q,b}\;\lesssim\;\mathrm{e}^{6\pi\kappa}\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi(\kappa+1)q}\Big|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big|^{2}.

(6.6)

3.

For
|y|<b
, the averaged part of
g_{q}(\cdot,\;y)
satisfies

|g_{q}^{*}(y)|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\;\sup_{\Im x=0}|g_{q}^{\bullet}(x,y)|.

(6.7)

4.

Writing

f_{1}(x):=\frac{1}{(1+\dot{\phi}(x))(1+\dot{\phi}(x+\omega))}-1,

(6.8)

we have the comparison estimate

\Big\|\Big\langle g_{q}(\cdot,0)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega\Big)^{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}}\Big\rangle_{q}^{N}\Big\|\;\lesssim\;\kappa\,\mathrm{e}^{4\pi\kappa}\,q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\Big|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big|^{2}.

(6.9)

5.

Let
S
be the normalized generating function associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in adapted coordinates (see the end of Section
5
), and define

S_{q}:=S\circ H_{q}.

Then

\bigg|S_{q}(x,y)-\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega+y\Big)^{2}\frac{1+f_{1}(x)}{2\bigl(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}\bigr)^{2}}\bigg|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}}\,\Big|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big|^{3},\qquad(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}_{a}\times\mathbb{D}_{b}.

(6.10)

Moreover, the change of coordinates
H_{q}
depends analytically on
\phi
and is real-analytic when
\phi
is real-valued.

Remark
.

The bound “
q
sufficiently large” depends only on
\nu
and
\kappa
.
In particular, it is independent of
(\omega,\phi)
.

Remark
.

The analytic dependence of
H_{q}
on
\phi
means more precisely as follows. Suppose
\phi^{\tau}(x)
is jointly analytic in
x\in\mathbb{T}_{1}
and a complex parameter
\tau
such that
\phi=\phi^{\tau}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
6.1
.
Then the corresponding coordinate transform
H_{q}=H^{\tau}_{q}
satisfies that
H_{q}^{\tau}(x,y)
is jointly analytic in
(\tau,x,y)
.

In the rest of this paper, we adopt the following terminology.
Given a standard map
F
associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in adapted coordinates and a
q
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
6.1
, we call the coordinates defined by
H_{q}
the
q
–resonant coordinates
.
We call the function
g_{q}
in (
6.4
) the
q
–resonant normal form
of
F
.
More generally, for a function
g
we call
\langle g\rangle_{q}^{N}
the
(q,N)
–resonant part
(or simply the
resonant part
) of
g
, and we call
g-\langle g\rangle_{q}^{N}
the
nonresonant part
.
We call the map
F_{q}=H_{q}^{-1}\circ F\circ H_{q}
the
standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in
q
-resonant coordinates
.

The proof of Theorem
6.1
is rather technical and will be given in Section
9
.

7
Controlling resonant parts by Picard iteration

We shall apply Theorem
6.1
to a sequence of coprime pairs of integers
(p_{j},q_{j})
with
q_{j}>0
such that
p_{j}/q_{j}
approximates
\omega
sufficiently fast, obtaining accordingly a sequence of resonant normal forms
g_{q_{j}}
from Theorem
6.1
.
With
N_{j}=\kappa q_{j}
for a fixed
\kappa
, we shall approximate the zero-average
q_{j}
-resonant part
\langle g^{\bullet}_{q_{j}}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
by the corresponding
q_{j}
-resonant part
\langle\phi\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
of the function
\phi
which defines the standard map.
Then, using a Picard iteration we find a choice of
\phi
for which the individual resonant part
\langle g^{\bullet}_{q_{j}}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
of the map takes desired forms.

Remark
.

To prove the results in this section, the only conclusions needed from Theorem
6.1
are (
6.9
) and the analytic dependence of
g_{q}
on
\phi
.
The other conclusions of Theorem
6.1
are not used in this Section.

We begin by defining certain Banach spaces suitably adapted to our problem.
Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the Banach space of analytic functions on
\mathbb{T}_{1}
with zero average and finite
\|\cdot\|
-norm.
For an integer
n>0
, define a Banach subspace
\mathcal{A}_{n}\subset\mathcal{A}
by

\mathcal{A}_{n}=\{u\in\mathcal{A}\mid\hat{u}_{k}=0\text{ for all }|k|<n\}.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the subspace of
\mathcal{A}
consisting of functions
u
such that
\dddot{u}\in\mathcal{A}
and put
\|u\|^{\prime}:=\|\dddot{u}\|
.
Since elements of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
have zero average, the space
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a Banach space with respect to the
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
-norm.
Similarly, we also define the Banach subspaces
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathcal{A}_{n}\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with induced
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
-norm.

We now state the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.1
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that the following holds.
For any
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and any sequence
(p_{j},q_{j})
of coprime integer pairs satisfying, for some
\nu\geq 70
, that

\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|\leq q_{j}^{-\nu},\quad q_{j+1}>2\kappa q_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad q_{j}>n,

(7.1)

and for any
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}
and
h\in\mathcal{A}
such that
h=\sum_{j}\left\langle{h}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
with
N_{j}=\kappa q_{j}
and

\|h\|+2\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}<1-2\epsilon,

(7.2)

there exists a unique analytic function
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}
depending analytically on
(h,\phi_{0})
such that
\phi_{1}=\sum_{j}\langle\phi_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
,
\|\phi_{1}\|^{\prime}\leq 1-\epsilon-\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}
and

\bigg\langle\frac{\partial g_{q_{j}}}{\partial x}(\cdot,0)\bigg\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}=\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Big)^{2}\left\langle{h}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\quad\text{for}\quad j\geq 1

(7.3)

where
g_{q_{j}}
is the
q_{j}
-resonant normal form as constructed in Theorem
6.1
with
\phi:=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}
and the chosen
\omega
,
\kappa
and
\nu
.

Remark
.

The meaning of analytic dependence of
\phi_{1}
on
(h,\phi_{0})
is in the sense analogous to the second remark following Theorem
6.1
, i.e., if
h^{\tau}(x)
and
\phi_{0}^{\tau}(x)
are both analytic jointly in
x\in\mathbb{T}_{1}
and a complex parameter
\tau
such that
h=h^{\tau}
and
\phi_{0}=\phi_{0}^{\tau}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
7.1
, then the corresponding function
\phi_{1}=\phi_{1}^{\tau}
has the property that
\phi_{1}^{\tau}(x)
is jointly analytic in
x
and
\tau
.

Theorem
7.1
will be proved by combining the usual Picard iteration (Banach fixed point theorem) with the quantitative estimates from the following lemma.

Lemma 7.2
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that the following holds. Let
\omega
and
(p_{j},q_{j})
be as in Theorem
7.1
such that (
7.1
) holds for some
\nu\geq 66
.
For any
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
with
\|\phi\|^{\prime}\leq 1
,
let

F_{q_{j}}:\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x+p_{j}/q_{j}+y\\
y+g_{q_{j}}(x,y)\end{pmatrix},\qquad g_{q_{j}}:\mathbb{T}_{1-3/q_{j}}\times\mathbb{D}_{|\omega-p_{j}/q_{j}|/5}\to\mathbb{C}

be the standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in the
q_{j}
-resonant coordinates as constructed by applying Theorem
6.1
with
q=q_{j}
and define

\mathcal{P}(\phi):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\langle{\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Big)^{-2}\frac{\partial g_{q_{j}}}{\partial x}(\cdot,0)-\dddot{\phi}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\quad\text{where}\quad N_{j}=\kappa q_{j}.

(7.4)

Then it holds that
\mathcal{P}(\phi):\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is an analytic function with

\|\mathcal{P}(\phi)\|<\epsilon,

(7.5)

and the operator
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
is analytic.
Moreover, for
\|\phi\|^{\prime}<1
, the operator norm of the derivative
d_{\phi}\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
satisfies

\|d_{\phi}\mathcal{P}\|<\frac{\epsilon}{1-\|\phi\|^{\prime}}.

(7.6)

Proof of Theorem
7.1
assuming Lemma
7.2
.

Let
n
be given by Lemma
7.2
and take
\omega
and
(p_{j},q_{j})
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
7.1
.
Note that the required conclusion (
7.3
) is equivalent to

\dddot{\phi}_{1}+\sum_{j\geq 1}\langle\dddot{\phi}_{0}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}=h-\mathcal{P}(\phi_{0}+\phi_{1})

(7.7)

where
\mathcal{P}
is as defined in (
7.4
).
In order to cast the problem into a fixed point problem, let
\mathscr{D}:u\mapsto\dot{u}
denote the derivative map.
Then
\mathscr{D}^{3}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
is a Banach space isometry.
Take the inverse
\mathscr{D}^{-3}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}:\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}

(7.8)

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{-3}\big[h-\mathcal{P}(\phi_{0}+\phi_{1})-\sum_{j\geq 1}\langle\dddot{\phi}_{0}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\big].

Then finding
\phi_{1}
satisfying (
7.7
) is equivalent to finding a fixed point of
\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}
.

Fix
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}
and
h\in\mathcal{A}
satisfying (
7.2
) and fix
r=1-\epsilon-\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}
.

Then, for
\|\phi_{1}\|^{\prime}\leq r
we have
\|\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}\|^{\prime}\leq 1-\epsilon<1
.
By the definition of
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
-norm, the estimate (
7.5
) of Lemma
7.2
and the assumption (
7.2
),

\|\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}(\phi_{1})\|^{\prime}\leq\|h\|+\|\mathcal{P}(\phi_{0}+\phi_{1})\|+\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}<\|h\|+\epsilon+\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}<r.

Taking the derivative of (
7.8
) in
\phi_{1}
and using the linearity of
\mathscr{D}^{-3}
, we have
d_{\phi_{1}}\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}=-\mathscr{D}^{-3}\circ d_{\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}}\mathcal{P}
and hence we deduce from (
7.6
) and
\|\phi_{1}\|^{\prime}\leq r
that the operator norm of the linear map
d_{\phi_{1}}\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}
satisfies

\|d_{\phi_{1}}\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}\|=\sup_{\|\delta\|^{\prime}=1}\|\mathscr{D}^{-3}[d_{\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}}\mathcal{P}(\delta)]\|^{\prime}=\|d_{\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}}\mathcal{P}\|<\frac{\epsilon}{1-\|\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}\|^{\prime}}\leq 1.

Note also that by definition of
\mathcal{P}
and the condition on
h
, we have
\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}(\phi_{1})=\sum_{j\geq 1}\langle\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}(\phi_{1})\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
.
Hence, the contracting mapping principle applies to
\mathcal{H}_{h,\phi_{0}}
on the closed set

\bigg\{u\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}\ :\ \|u\|^{\prime}\leq r,\ u=\sum_{j\geq 1}\langle u\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\bigg\}

and yields a unique fixed point therein.
Moreover, by analytic dependence of
\mathcal{P}
on
\phi
as shown in Lemma
7.2
, the fixed point depends analytically on
h
and
\phi_{0}
.
The proof is finished.
∎

In the rest of this section, we focus on the proof of Lemma
7.2
.
Recall that Theorem
6.1
gives a comparison estimate (
6.9
).
The main idea for the proof of Lemma
7.2
will be to give a similar comparison estimate between the resonant part
\langle\dddot{\phi}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
of
\dddot{\phi}
and the function

-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\bigg\langle\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}\bigg\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}.

Since
f_{1}
depends explicitly on
\phi
via (
6.8
), we can compute an estimate directly.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be given.
Consider a sequence
(p_{j},q_{j})
of coprime integer pairs with
q_{j}>0
satisfying the assumptions (
7.1
) with some
n>0
to be fixed later and
\nu\geq 70
.
Taking
n
sufficiently large, we can apply Theorem
6.1
to any
\phi\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with
\|\phi\|^{\prime}\leq 1
and each
q_{j}
and accordingly we can define
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
as in (
7.4
) using the sequence
(g_{q_{j}})_{j\geq 1}
of
q_{j}
-resonant normal forms furnished by Theorem
6.1
.
We will take
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
.
To study the series on the right hand side of the definition (
7.4
), we shall take
M_{j}=2N_{j}=2\kappa q_{j}
and decompose
\mathcal{P}(\phi)=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(\phi)+\sum_{j\geq 1}\big(\mathcal{P}_{j}(\phi)+\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{j}(\phi)\big)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(\phi)=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j\geq 1}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}\ddot{f}_{1}+\dddot{\phi}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{j}(\phi)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}-\ddot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}(\phi)=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left\langle{\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Big)^{-2}g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}

and
f_{1}:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is as defined in (
6.8
).
We shall analyse
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{j}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{j}
separately.

We start with
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
.
By definition of
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
-norm, the assumptions
\|\phi\|^{\prime}\leq 1
and
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
imply

\|\ddot{\phi}\|=\sum_{|k|\geq n}(2\pi|k|)^{2}|\hat{\phi}_{k}|e^{2\pi|k|}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi n}\sum_{|k|\geq n}(2\pi|k|)^{3}|\hat{\phi}_{k}|e^{2\pi|k|}=\frac{\|\phi\|^{\prime}}{2\pi n}.

Similarly, we have
\|\dot{\phi}\|\leq(2\pi n)^{-2}
.
From the explicit expression
(1+f_{1})=((1+\dot{\phi})(1+\dot{\phi}^{+}))^{-1}
, a direct calculation shows that

\frac{1}{2}f_{1}+\dot{\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\phi}=\frac{1}{2}(f_{1}+\dot{\phi}+\dot{\phi}^{+})=\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}+(\dot{\phi}^{+})^{2}+\dot{\phi}\dot{\phi}^{+}+\dot{\phi}^{2}\dot{\phi}^{+}+\dot{\phi}(\dot{\phi}^{+})^{2}}{2(1+\dot{\phi})(1+\dot{\phi}^{+})}.

By differentiating the above expression twice and using
|\dot{\phi}|\leq(2\pi n)^{-2}
,
|\ddot{\phi}|\leq(2\pi n)^{-1}
and
\|\dddot{\phi}\|\leq 1
, it is straightforward to verify that the right hand side of the above equation has
\|\cdot\|
-norm bounded by
\lesssim 1/n
.
On the other hand, by Lemma
C.2
,

\|\langle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dddot{\phi}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\|=\|\langle\mathcal{D}_{\omega-p_{j}/q_{j}}\dddot{\phi}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\|\leq\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|\left\|\Big\langle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\dddot{\phi}\Big\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\right\|\lesssim N_{j}\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|\|\dddot{\phi}\|.

Thus, using
N_{j}=\kappa q_{j}
we obtain

\|\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(\phi)\|\lesssim\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{j}N_{j}\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|\|\dddot{\phi}\|\lesssim\frac{1}{n}+\kappa\sum_{j}q_{j}\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|.

Since
|\omega-p_{j}/q_{j}|\leq q_{j}^{-\nu}
and
q_{j+1}>2\kappa q_{j}>n
, we can bound
q_{j}|\omega-p_{j}/q_{j}|<[(2\kappa)^{j-1}n]^{-\nu+1}
and then bound the series above by a converging geometric series to deduce

\|\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(\phi)\|\lesssim\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\kappa n^{-\nu+1}}{1-(2\kappa)^{-\nu+1}}.

(7.9)

Next, we consider
\mathcal{P}_{j}
.
By a straightforward computation, we have

\mathcal{P}_{j}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\frac{(\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}})^{2}}{(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}})^{2}}+\frac{\langle\ddot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}.

Using
\|\dot{\phi}\|\leq(2\pi n)^{-2}
and the formula (
6.8
) for
f_{1}
, the denominators above are uniformly bounded away from
0
.
Since
\|\dot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim\|\ddot{\phi}\|
and
\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim\|\dddot{\phi}\|\leq 1
,

\|\mathcal{P}_{j}(\phi)\|\lesssim\|\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}\|.

By assumption
q_{j+1}>M_{j}
, the orders of nonzero Fourier coefficients of
\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}
are
disjoint
subsets of
\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence, it follows from the definition of
\|\cdot\|
-norm and the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{j}
that

\Big\|\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathcal{P}_{j}(\phi)\Big\|=\sum_{j\geq 1}\|\mathcal{P}_{j}(\phi)\|\lesssim\sum_{j\geq 1}\|\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}\|\lesssim\|\dot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim\|\ddot{\phi}\|\lesssim\frac{1}{n}.

(7.10)

To analyse
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{j}(\phi)
, we start with the following decomposition

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{j}(\phi)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left\langle{\Big(\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big)^{-2}g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left\langle{\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}-\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}.

By the estimate (
6.9
) of Theorem
6.1
and
N_{j}=\kappa q_{j}
, the first term is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left\langle{\Big(\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big)^{-2}g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\right\|

(7.11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\pi N_{j}\left\|\left\langle{\Big(\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big)^{-2}g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}\mathrm{e}^{4\pi\kappa}q_{j}^{\nu_{0}-\nu+1}

with
\nu_{0}=64
as in Theorem
6.1
,
whereas the second term writes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left\langle{\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}-\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\left\langle{\log(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}})-\log(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}})}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle{\frac{\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{>M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}+t\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{>M_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}dt.

By the first statement of Lemma
C.3
and the definition
M_{j}=2N_{j}=2\kappa q_{j}
,

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\left\langle{\frac{\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{>M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}+t\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{>M_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\Bigg\|\leq

\displaystyle(2\kappa+1)\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi(M_{j}-N_{j})}\Bigg\|\frac{1}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}+t\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{>M_{j}}}\Bigg\|\|\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{>M_{j}}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\kappa\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi\kappa q_{j}}.

Substituting into the integral above, we obtain

\left\|\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left\langle{\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{M_{j}}}-\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}}\right\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\right\|\lesssim N_{j}^{2}\kappa\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi\kappa q_{j}}\lesssim\kappa^{3}q_{j}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi\kappa q_{j}}.

Combining with (
7.11
) and summing over
j\geq 1
, we obtain

\Big\|\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{j}(\phi)\Big\|\lesssim\sum_{j\geq 1}\kappa^{2}\mathrm{e}^{4\pi\kappa}q_{j}^{\nu_{0}-\nu+1}+\kappa^{3}q_{j}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi\kappa q_{j}}.

Using the assumption
\nu\geq\nu_{0}+6
and
q_{j}>(2\kappa)^{j-1}n
and bounding the series in the right hand side above by convergent geometric series,

\Big\|\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{j}(\phi)\Big\|\lesssim\frac{\kappa^{2}\mathrm{e}^{4\pi\kappa}}{1-(2\kappa)^{-\nu+\nu_{0}+1}}n^{-\nu+\nu_{0}+1}+\kappa^{3}n^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi\kappa n}.

(7.12)

Consolidating (
7.9
), (
7.10
) and (
7.12
), we arrive at

\|\mathcal{P}(\phi)\|\lesssim\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\kappa^{2}\mathrm{e}^{4\pi\kappa}}{1-(2\kappa)^{-\nu+\nu_{0}+1}}n^{-\nu+\nu_{0}+1}+\kappa^{3}n^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi\kappa n}.

(7.13)

In particular, the right hand side of (
7.4
) is an absolutely convergent series in
\mathcal{A}
. Thus
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
is well-defined as an element in
\mathcal{A}
.
Taking
n
sufficiently big, we can make the right hand side of (
7.13
) smaller than any given positive number and thus obtain (
7.5
).
Since
\nu_{0}=64
and we assume
\nu\geq 70
, we have
n^{-\nu+\nu_{0}+1}\leq n^{-5}
and thus the ‘sufficiently big’ condition on
n
depends only on
\epsilon
and
\kappa
, not on
\nu
.

Next, we consider the analyticity
\phi\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\phi)
.
Let
\phi^{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
depend analytically on some complex parameter
\tau\in\mathbb{C}
.
Suppose
\|\phi^{\tau}\|^{\prime}\leq 1
for all
|\tau|\leq R
so that
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\tau})
is defined.
We consider
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\tau})
as the point-wise limit of the partial sum of its Fourier series

\mathcal{P}^{K}(\phi^{\tau})(x):=\sum_{|k|\leq K}\widehat{\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\tau})}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx}.

Indeed, by the absolute convergence of (
7.4
) established above, if
|\tau|\leq R
then the sequence
\mathcal{P}^{K}(\phi^{\tau})
converges in
\mathcal{A}
as
K\to\infty
.
In particular, for each fixed
\tau
, the set
\{\mathcal{P}^{K}(\phi^{\tau})\mid K\geq 1\}
is a relatively compact subset in
\mathcal{A}
.
On the other hand, the function
\mathcal{P}^{K}(\phi^{\tau}):\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
has finitely many nonzero Fourier coefficients and, by analytic dependence of
\langle g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}
on
\phi^{\tau}
, each of these coefficients depends analytically on
\tau
.
It follows that
\mathcal{P}^{K}(\phi^{\tau})
is an analytic function in
\tau
taking values in
\mathcal{A}
.
Hence, by Montel’s theorem, the family of analytic functions
\{\tau\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{K}(\phi^{\tau})\}_{K\geq 1}
is equicontinuous, and, by Ascoli’s theorem, the convergence
\mathcal{P}^{K}(\phi^{\tau})\to\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\tau})
as
K\to\infty
is uniform over
\tau
.
It follows that
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\tau})
depends analytically in
\tau
since it is the uniform limit of a sequence of analytic functions.
We have thus shown that
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
is analytic.

Finally, we show (
7.6
).
Put
\phi^{\tau}=\phi+\tau\delta
for some
\phi,\delta\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}
with
\|\phi\|^{\prime}<1
.
Then we have that
\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\tau})
is analytic for
|\tau|<\frac{1-\|\phi\|^{\prime}}{\|\delta\|^{\prime}}
.
Applying Cauchy’s estimate to the derivative
\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathcal{P}(\phi^{\tau})|_{\tau=0}
and using (
7.5
), we obtain

\|d_{\phi}\mathcal{P}(\delta)\|<\Big(\frac{1-\|\phi\|^{\prime}}{\|\delta\|^{\prime}}\Big)^{-1}\epsilon=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\|\phi\|^{\prime}}\|\delta\|^{\prime}.

Since
\delta
can be chosen arbitrarily from an open ball, it follows that
\|d_{\phi}\mathcal{P}\|\leq\epsilon(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime})^{-1}
in operator norm.
The proof of the Lemma is finished.
∎

Remark
.

In the proof of Theorem
7.1
, one can avoid dealing with analytic functions taking values in an infinite dimensional Banach space by employing a slightly different argument.
Let
\hat{u}_{k}(z)
be the
k
-th Fourier coefficient of
\mathcal{P}(\phi+z\delta)
where
\phi,\delta\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
\|\phi\|^{\prime}+\|\delta\|^{\prime}<1
.
By analytic dependence of
g_{q_{j}}
’s, each
\hat{u}_{k}(z)
is an analytic function in
z
for
\|\phi+z\delta\|^{\prime}<1
.
Let us denote

G(\phi,\delta):x\mapsto\sum_{k}\hat{u}_{k}^{\prime}(0)e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kx}.

We shall prove that
G(\phi,\cdot):\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
is a bounded linear map and it is in fact equal to
\mathrm{d}_{\phi}\mathcal{P}
.

Fix
\theta_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|\hat{u}_{k}(1)-\hat{u}_{k}(0)-\hat{u}_{k}^{\prime}(0)|=(\hat{u}_{k}(1)-\hat{u}_{k}(0)-\hat{u}_{k}^{\prime}(0))e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{k}}
.
Consider

H(z)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{u}_{k}(z)e^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{k}}e^{2\pi|k|}.

(7.14)

By the same argument using Montel’s theorem and Ascoli’s theorem as in the proof of Lemma
7.2
, one can show that the series (
7.14
) converges uniformly on compact subsets of the open disc of radius
(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime})/\|\delta\|^{\prime}
and therefore defines a complex analytic function in
z
, which is uniformly bounded by
\epsilon
.
Similarly, the function
z\mapsto\sum_{k}\hat{u}_{k}(z)e^{2\pi|k|}
is analytic and uniformly bounded by
\epsilon
over the same domain.
Then, by Cauchy’s estimate we have
\|G(\phi,\delta)\|\leq\epsilon(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime})^{-1}\|\delta\|^{\prime}
.
Hence,
G(\phi,\cdot)
is bounded in operator norm by
\epsilon(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime})^{-1}
.
Next, by the choice of
\theta_{k}
, the mean value theorem and Cauchy’s estimate,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}(\phi+\delta)-\mathcal{P}(\phi)-G(\phi,\delta)\|=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}|\hat{u}_{k}(1)-\hat{u}_{k}(0)-\hat{u}_{k}^{\prime}(0)|e^{2\pi|k|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}(\hat{u}_{k}(1)-\hat{u}_{k}(0)-\hat{u}_{k}^{\prime}(0))\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{k}}e^{2\pi|k|}

\displaystyle=
