\displaystyle H(1)-H(0)-H^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}H^{\prime\prime}(t)(1-t)dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{2(\frac{1-\|\phi\|^{\prime}}{\|\delta\|^{\prime}}-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon\|\delta\|^{\prime 2}}{(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime}-\|\delta\|^{\prime})^{2}}.

Assuming
\|\delta\|^{\prime}
is sufficiently small (
\|\delta\|^{\prime}<(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime})/2
suffices), we obtain
\|\mathcal{P}(\phi+\delta)-\mathcal{P}(\phi)-G(\phi,\delta)\|\lesssim\epsilon(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime})^{-2}\|\delta\|^{\prime 2}
.
This shows that
G(\phi,\cdot)
is indeed the derivative
d_{\phi}\mathcal{P}
and thus we have
\|d_{\phi}\mathcal{P}\|\leq\epsilon(1-\|\phi\|^{\prime})^{-1}
as required.

8
Analysis of almost equidistributed periodic orbits: the end of the proof of Theorem
1.3

We will apply Theorem
7.1
to construct a sequence of
(p_{j},q_{j})
-periodic orbits of a standard map with well-controlled symplectic actions and eigendata, and then complete the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

More precisely, we start with the following lemma.
We recall that the functional space
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
and the norm
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
have been defined at the beginning of Section
7
.

Lemma 8.1
.

The following statements hold for all sufficiently large
n>0
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and assume there exists a sequence

(p_{j},q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of coprime integer pairs such that, for some

\nu\geq 70
,

\Bigl|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Bigr|\leq q_{j}^{-\nu},\qquad q_{j+1}>4q_{j},\qquad q_{1}>n.

(8.1)

Then, for every
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
and every sequence

(\sigma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of complex numbers satisfying

|\sigma_{j}|q_{j}\geq 1\quad\text{for all }j\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{j\geq 1}|\sigma_{j}|+2\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2},

there exists
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
, depending analytically on
\phi_{0}
and on

(\sigma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, with the following properties.

Let
\phi:=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}
and let
F
be the standard map associated
with
(\omega,\phi)
in the adapted coordinates.

For each
j\geq 1
there exist two distinguished
(p_{j},q_{j})
-periodic orbits of
F
in a complex neighborhood of
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. These orbits depend analytically on
\phi_{0}
and
(\sigma_{j})_{j\geq 1}

and satisfy:

1.

The two orbits are geometrically distinct.
If
(\sigma_{j})_{j}
and
\phi_{0}
are real valued, then the two orbits lie in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
and they are the
only

(p_{j},q_{j})
-periodic orbits of
F
in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
.
In particular, one of them is a (globally) minimal orbit and the other
is a minimax orbit.

2.

Let
S
be the normalized generating function associated with
F
in adapted coordinates, and
define the rescaled symplectic actions of the two
(p_{j},q_{j})
-orbits by

\mathcal{A}_{j,\pm}:=\frac{1}{q_{j}(\omega-p_{j}/q_{j})^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{q_{j}-1}S\circ F^{k}(\xi_{j,\pm}),

(8.2)

where
\xi_{j,+}
and
\xi_{j,-}
are any points on the two orbits,
respectively.
Then

\Biggl|\mathcal{A}_{j,\pm}-\frac{1}{2(1+f_{1}^{*})}\Bigl(1\mp\frac{2\sigma_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}}{(2\pi q_{j})^{2}}\Bigr)\Biggr|\;\lesssim\;q_{j}^{\nu_{0}}\Bigl|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Bigr|+q_{j}^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}},

(8.3)

where
f_{1}:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is defined in terms of
\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}

as in (
6.8
),
f_{1}^{*}
denotes its average over
\mathbb{T}
, and
\nu_{0}=64
.

3.

Define the rescaled Lyapunov exponents of these orbits by

\Lambda_{j,\pm}:=-\frac{1}{q_{j}^{2}(\omega-p_{j}/q_{j})^{2}}\det\bigl(d_{\xi_{j,\pm}}F^{q_{j}}-\id\bigr),

(8.4)

where
\xi_{j,\pm}
are as in
(2)
.
Then

\bigl|\Lambda_{j,\pm}\mp\sigma_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\bigr|<\frac{1}{50}|\sigma_{j}|\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}.

(8.5)

The main theorem will be deduced from Lemma
8.1
together with the following elementary lemma.

To state Lemma
8.2
, we denote by
\ell^{\infty}
the Banach space of bounded sequences
\beta=(\beta_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of complex numbers with the supremum norm

|\beta|_{\ell^{\infty}}:=\sup_{j\geq 1}|\beta_{j}|.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
B
be the unit open ball of
\ell^{\infty}
centered at
0
. Let
T:B\to\ell^{\infty}
be an analytic map. Suppose

|T(\beta)-\beta|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\epsilon\qquad\text{for all}\quad\beta\in B.

(8.6)

Then, for any
\alpha\in\ell^{\infty}
such that
|\alpha|_{\ell^{\infty}}<(1-4\epsilon)/3
, there exists a unique
\beta_{*}\in\ell^{\infty}
depending analytically on
\alpha
such that
|\beta_{*}|_{\ell^{\infty}}<(1-\epsilon)/3
and
T(\beta_{*})=\alpha
.
Furthermore, if
\mathcal{U}
is an open subset of a complex Banach space and the map
T=T_{u}
is analytically parametrized by
u\in\mathcal{U}
, i.e., the value
T_{u}(\beta)_{j}
is analytic in
u
and
\beta\in B
for each
j
, then the corresponding
\beta_{*}
such that
T_{u}(\beta_{*})=\alpha
also depends analytically on
u
.

Remark
.

We say that a map
T:U\to\ell^{\infty}
defined on an open subset
U\subset\ell^{\infty}
is
analytic
if the following property holds: for any family
(b^{\tau})
of elements in
U
parametrized by a complex parameter
\tau
such that, for each index
j\geq 1
, the function
\tau\mapsto b_{j}^{\tau}
is analytic, the function
\tau\mapsto T(b^{\tau})_{j}
is analytic for every index
j
.

Proof of Lemma
8.2
.

We prove this lemma by reducing it to a fixed-point problem and solving it by the usual Picard iteration.
Let
\alpha\in\ell^{\infty}
be given. Define an auxiliary map

\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}:\beta\mapsto\alpha-T(\beta)+\beta.

Then finding
\beta
solving the equation
T(\beta)=\alpha
is equivalent to finding a fixed point of
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}
.

By the assumption (
8.6
), we have
|{\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}(\beta)}|_{\ell^{\infty}}<|{\alpha}|_{\ell^{\infty}}+\epsilon
.
Hence, for
|{\alpha}|_{\ell^{\infty}}+\epsilon\leq r<1
, the map
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}
preserves the closed ball of radius
r
centered at
0
.
Pick any
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\ell^{\infty}
such that
|{\beta}|_{\ell^{\infty}}+|{\beta^{\prime}}|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1
.
Then by the mean value theorem applied to the function
\tau\mapsto\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}((1-\tau)\beta+\tau\beta^{\prime})_{j}
for an arbitrary
j\geq 1
,

\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}(\beta^{\prime})_{j}-\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}(\beta)_{j}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}((1-\tau)\beta+\tau\beta^{\prime})_{j}d\tau.

By (
8.6
) and Cauchy’s estimate over the domain
|\tau|\leq(1-|{\beta}|_{\ell^{\infty}})/|{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}|_{\ell^{\infty}}
,

\left|{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}((1-\tau)\beta+\tau\beta^{\prime})_{j}}\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon|{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}|_{\ell^{\infty}}}{1-|{\beta}|_{\ell^{\infty}}-|{\beta^{\prime}-\beta}|_{\ell^{\infty}}}\qquad\text{for}\quad|\tau|\leq 1.

Since the above bound is independent of
j
, we deduce

|{\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}(\beta^{\prime})-\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}(\beta)}|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq\frac{\epsilon|{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}|_{\ell^{\infty}}}{1-|{\beta}|_{\ell^{\infty}}-|{\beta^{\prime}-\beta}|_{\ell^{\infty}}}.

In particular, for
r<1/3
, the Lipschitz constant of
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}
is at most
\epsilon/(1-3r)
.
By the condition on
\alpha
, there exists
r
such that
|\alpha|+\epsilon\leq r<(1-\epsilon)/3
.
It follows that the map
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}
preserves the closed ball of radius
r
and is a contracting map there.
By Banach fixed point theorem, we obtain a unique fixed point for
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}
therein.

It remains to show the analytic dependence of
\beta
on
\alpha
and on
u\in\mathcal{U}
, if
T=T_{u}
is analytically parametrized by
u
belonging to an open subset of a complex Banach space.
Recall that, by the usual proof of Banach fixed point theorem, the fixed point
\beta_{*}
can be given by the limit
\beta_{*}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}^{n}(0)
, where the contracting property of
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}
implies that
(\widetilde{T}_{\alpha}^{n}(0))_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\ell^{\infty}
.
On the other hand, if
\alpha^{\tau}
depends analytically on a complex parameter
\tau
in a domain of
\mathbb{C}
and has small
|{\ \cdot\ }|_{\ell^{\infty}}
-norm uniformly in
\tau
, and
T
is analytically parametrized by
u
,
then, by the assumed analyticity of
T
, for fixed
j
the sequence
\{\widetilde{T}_{\alpha^{\tau}}^{n}(0)_{j}\ :\ n\geq 1\}
is a Cauchy sequence (with respect to uniform norm in
\tau
) of analytic functions in
\tau
and
u
.
By standard results in complex analysis, the limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{T}_{\alpha^{\tau}}^{n}(0)_{j}
is an analytic function in
\tau
and
u
.
It follows that
\beta_{*}
is analytic in
\alpha
and
u
.
The proof of the Lemma is finished.
∎

8.1
Proof of the main theorem.

We now complete the proof of the main theorem using Lemma
8.1
and Lemma
8.2
.
Let
\omega
and
(p_{j},q_{j})
be given as in the statement of Theorem
1.3
.
In particular, we may assume
q_{1}>n
so that Lemma
8.1
applies to the sequence
(p_{j},q_{j})
.
We consider

\sigma_{j}=\frac{2+\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}{q_{j}}\quad\text{with}\quad|\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}|\leq 1.

This choice of
\sigma_{j}
automatically ensures that
q_{j}|\sigma_{j}|\geq 1
.
Moreover, for
q_{1}
sufficiently large we have
\sum_{j}|\sigma_{j}|<1/10
.
It follows that the condition
\sum_{j}|\sigma_{j}|+2\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}\leq 1/2
is satisfied whenever
\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}\leq 1/5
.
In this case, by Lemma
8.1
there exists
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
depending analytically on
(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})
and
\phi_{0}
such that conclusions of Lemma
8.1
hold.
In particular, for each
j\geq 1
there is a pair of
(p_{j},q_{j})
-orbits depending analytically on
(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})
and
\phi_{0}
.
Let
\xi_{j,\pm}
be points picked from the two distinguished
(p_{j},q_{j})
-orbits furnished by Lemma
8.1
, respectively.
We shall verify that the conclusions of Theorem
1.3
hold with respect to the sequence of orbits
\xi_{j}=\xi_{j,+}
provided by Lemma
8.1
.
We first prove the simpler part (2) before proceeding to part (1).

Proof of part (2).

Let
\Lambda_{j}=\Lambda_{j,+}
with
\Lambda_{j,+}
defined as in (
8.4
).
We provisionally define

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j}=q_{j}\Lambda_{j}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi q_{j}}-2.

By analytic dependence of
\phi_{1}
and
\xi_{j}
on
(\sigma_{j})
and
\phi_{0}
, the map
T:(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})_{j}\mapsto(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j})_{j}
is analytic and is analytically parametrized by
\phi_{0}
.
On the other hand, the estimate (
8.5
) and the choice of
\sigma_{j}
implies

\left|{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}\right|<\frac{1}{16}.

We may in turn apply Lemma
8.2
with
\epsilon=1/16
and

T:(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})_{j}\mapsto(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j})_{j}
to conclude that, for all sequence
(\alpha_{j})_{j}
with
|\alpha_{j}|<1/4
and all
\phi_{0}
such that
\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}\leq 1/5
, there exists a unique sequence
(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})_{j}
with
|\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}|<5/16
and depending analytically on
(\alpha_{j})
and
\phi_{0}
such that
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j}=\alpha_{j}
.
By Lemma
8.1
, the function
\phi_{1}
realizing the prescribed values of
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{j}
can be chosen to depend analytically on
(\alpha_{j})
and
\phi_{0}
.
It now follows from (
8.5
) that the
q_{j}
-periodic orbits
(\xi_{j})
has a Lyapunov exponent given by

\det(\mathrm{d}_{\xi_{j}}F^{q_{j}}-\id)=-(2+\alpha_{j})q_{j}\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Big)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}.

Since the Lyapunov exponent is obviously independent of coordinates, we deduce (
1.8
), where the potential
V_{\alpha}
is defined by (
5.3
) with
\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}
and the choice of additive constant fixed by the normalization condition on
S
.

Proof of part (1).

We impose the following arithmetic condition as in (
1.9
)

\left|{\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}}\right|\leq q_{j}^{-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\quad\text{with}\quad\nu=70.

(8.7)

Let us write
\mathcal{A}_{j}=\mathcal{A}_{j,+}
with
\mathcal{A}_{j,+}
as in (
8.2
) and provisionally define

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{j}:=2\pi^{2}q_{j}^{3}\bigl[1-2(1+f_{1}^{*})\mathcal{A}_{j}\bigr]\mathrm{e}^{2\pi q_{j}}-2.

By analyticity we have similarly that
T:(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})_{j}\mapsto(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{j})_{j}
is an analytic map.
Combining the arithmetic condition (
8.7
) with (
8.3
),

\left|{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{j}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}\right|\lesssim q_{j}^{\nu_{0}+3-\nu}\lesssim\frac{1}{q_{j}^{3}}

In particular, if
q_{1}
is large enough, then the right hand side above can be bounded uniformly in
j
by
<1/16
.
We may in turn apply Lemma
8.2
as in the preceding proof of part (2) to conclude that, for all sequence
(\alpha_{j})_{j}
with
|\alpha_{j}|<1/4
and all
\phi_{0}
such that
\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}\leq 1/5
, there exists a unique sequence
(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})_{j}
with
|\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}|<5/16
such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{j}=\alpha_{j}
.
By Lemma
8.1
, there exists
\phi_{1}
depending analytically on
(\alpha_{j})
and
\phi_{0}
such that the standard map
F
associated with
(\omega,\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1})
in adapted coordinates has a sequence of
q_{j}
-periodic orbits
(\xi_{j})
whose symplectic action with respect to the normalized generating function
S
in adapted coordinates is

\sum_{k=0}^{q_{j}-1}S\circ F^{k}(\xi_{j})=\frac{q_{j}\big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\big)^{2}}{2(1+f_{1}^{*})}\Big[1-\frac{(2+\alpha_{j})\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}}{2\pi^{2}q_{j}^{3}}\Big].

By (
5.10
), the symplectic action of
(\xi_{j})
with respect to the normalized generating function
\mathscr{S}
in the original coordinates is

\sum_{k=0}^{q_{j}-1}\mathscr{S}\circ\mathscr{H}\circ F^{k}(\xi_{j})=\sum_{k=0}^{q_{j}-1}S\circ F^{k}(\xi_{j})+\bigg(\omega-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi\dot{\phi}^{+}\bigg)p_{j}

where
\mathscr{H}
is the coordinate transform defined in (
5.4
).
Hence, we have deduced (
1.10
).

If
(\sigma_{j})
and
\phi_{0}
are real valued as assumed in Theorem
1.3
, then by Lemma
8.1
part (1), the standard map has only 2 geometrically distinct
(p_{j},q_{j})
-orbits, containing
\xi_{j,+}
and
\xi_{j,-}
respectively.
Since
\sigma_{j}>0
by our choice, it follows easily from (
8.3
) and the estimate (
8.7
) that the orbit of
\xi_{j,+}
has smaller symplectic action between the two, and hence
\xi_{j}=\xi_{j,+}
is a (globally) minimal orbit of rotation number
p_{j}/q_{j}
.

Finally, it remains to show that one can choose
V_{\alpha}
in such a way that the quantities
f_{1}^{*}
and
(\phi_{\alpha}\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+})^{*}
are both constant in
\alpha
.
The main idea is that both of two quantities are dominated by the low-frequency components in the Fourier series of
\phi
.
Hence, if
q_{1}
is sufficiently large compared to the order of nonzero Fourier coefficients of
\phi_{0}
, then the choice of
\phi_{0}
will have a dominant effect on
f_{1}^{*}
and
(\phi_{\alpha}\dot{\phi}_{\alpha}^{+})^{*}
.
By adjusting
\phi_{0}
, we can keep the two quantities constant while varying
\alpha
.

Let us formalize this argument.
We define a map

\mathcal{G}:(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\mapsto\big(f_{1}^{*},(\phi\dot{\phi}^{+})^{*}\big)

where
\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}
and
f_{1}
is defined as in (
6.8
).
In the preceding steps, we have constructed an analytic map
(\alpha,\phi_{0})\mapsto\phi_{1}
where the standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1})
has a sequence of closed orbits with specified symplectic actions.
Since
\phi_{1}
is determined by
(\alpha,\phi_{0})
, we consider
\mathcal{G}(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
as a function in terms
(\alpha,\phi_{0})
and we apply the implicit function theorem.
In fact, we pick a nonzero
\widetilde{\phi}_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
and consider the derivative

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{G}}{\mathrm{d}\phi_{0}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\phi_{0}}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\phi_{1}}\frac{\partial\phi_{1}}{\partial\phi_{0}}.

Pick
\widetilde{\phi}_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
such that the derivative
\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\phi_{0}}
is surjective onto
\mathbb{C}^{2}

and take
q_{1}\gg n
.
Since

\sup_{\Im x=0}|\phi_{1}(x)|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{1}},

it is easy to establish by Cauchy’s estimate that

\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\phi_{1}}\frac{\partial\phi_{1}}{\partial\phi_{0}}
has arbitrarily small operator norm as
q_{1}\to\infty
.
Hence, for
q_{1}
sufficiently large,
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{G}/\mathrm{d}\phi_{0}
is surjective and we can apply the implicit function theorem to obtain
\epsilon_{0}>0
and an analytic map
\alpha\mapsto\phi_{0}
such that
\mathcal{G}(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
is constant in
\alpha
for
|\alpha|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\epsilon_{0}
.

We have completed the proof of Theorem
1.3
using Lemma
8.1
.
We proceed to prove Lemma
8.1
now.

8.2
Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

From now on, we fix

\kappa=2.

Let
n>0
be given by Theorem
7.1
with
\epsilon=1/4
and
\kappa=2
.
Take
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and a sequence
(p_{j},q_{j})
such that (
8.1
) holds for some
\nu\geq 70
.
Let
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
and
(\sigma_{j})_{j}
be given as in Lemma
8.1
.
In particular, we assume

|\sigma_{j}|q_{j}\geq 1

and

\sum_{j}|\sigma_{j}|+2\|\phi_{0}\|^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

(8.8)

Application of Theorem
7.1
.

We apply Theorem
7.1
to the function
h\in\mathcal{A}
defined by

h(x):=\sum_{j\geq 1}\sigma_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\cos(2\pi q_{j}x).

(8.9)

Note that, by the definition of the Fourier-weighted norm,

\|h\|=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\sigma_{j}|.

By (
8.8
), the condition (
7.2
) of Theorem
7.1
is satisfied with the choice
\epsilon=1/4
.

Fix
N_{j}:=\kappa q_{j}=2q_{j}
.
Then, by Theorem
7.1
, there exists
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
depending analytically on
h
and
\phi_{0}
such that the standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi:=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1})
in the
q_{j}
–resonant coordinates can be written as

F_{q_{j}}(x,y)=\bigl(x+p_{j}/q_{j}+y,\;y+g_{q_{j}}(x,y)\bigr),

where the resonant normal form
g_{q_{j}}
satisfies (
7.3
).

For notational simplicity, put

\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}:=\langle g_{q_{j}}\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}.

By (
7.3
) and our choice of
h
,

\frac{\partial\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}}{\partial x}(x,0)=\sigma_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Big)^{2}\cos(2\pi q_{j}x).

(8.10)

Integrating (
8.10
) in
x
gives

\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}(x,0)=\frac{\sigma_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}}{2\pi q_{j}}\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Big)^{2}\sin(2\pi q_{j}x)\;+\;g_{q_{j}}^{*}(0),

(8.11)

where
g_{q_{j}}^{*}(0)
denotes the average of
x\mapsto g_{q_{j}}(x,0)
over
\mathbb{T}
.

By Theorem
6.1
, the functions
\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}
and
g_{q_{j}}
are defined on
\mathbb{T}_{a_{j}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b_{j}}
with

a_{j}=1-\frac{3}{q_{j}},\qquad b_{j}=\frac{1}{5}\Big|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big|.

The conclusions (
6.6
) and (
6.9
) respectively read as

\displaystyle|g_{q_{j}}-\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}|_{1/q_{j},b_{j}}\;\lesssim\;\mathrm{e}^{-6\pi q_{j}}\Big|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big|^{2},

(8.12)

\displaystyle\Big\|\Big\langle g_{q_{j}}(\cdot,0)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}-\omega\Big)^{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q_{j}}}\Big\rangle_{q_{j}}^{N_{j}}\Big\|\;\lesssim\;q_{j}^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\Big|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big|^{2},

(8.13)

where
f_{1}
is as in (
6.8
) with
\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}
, and we have absorbed the factor
\kappa\mathrm{e}^{4\pi\kappa}
into the implicit constant because
\kappa=2
is fixed.

Moreover, by (
6.7
), for
|y|<b_{j}
we have

|g_{q_{j}}^{*}(y)|\;\lesssim\;q_{j}^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\Big(\sup_{\Im x=0}|(g_{q_{j}}-\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}})(x,y)|+\sup_{\Im x=0}|\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}^{\bullet}(x,y)|\Big),

(8.14)

since

g_{q_{j}}^{\bullet}=g_{q_{j}}-g_{q_{j}}^{*}=\bigl(g_{q_{j}}-\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}\bigr)+\bigl(\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}-\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}^{*}\bigr)=\bigl(g_{q_{j}}-\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}\bigr)+\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}^{\bullet}

and
\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}^{*}=g_{q_{j}}^{*}
.

For
y=0
, we have an explicit formula (
8.11
) for
\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}(x,0)
.
Hence, using (
8.12
) and the fact that
|\sigma_{j}|\geq 1/q_{j}\geq\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q_{j}}
for
q_{j}
sufficiently large, we obtain

|g_{q_{j}}^{*}(0)|\;\lesssim\;|\sigma_{j}|\,q_{j}^{\nu_{0}-\nu-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\Big|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big|^{2}.

(8.15)

For general
|y|<b_{j}
,
we have, by
\kappa=2
and the estimate for Fourier coefficients,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}^{\bullet}|_{1/q_{j},b_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\pm\{q_{j},2q_{j}\}}\sup_{|y|<b_{j}}|\widehat{g}_{q_{j}}(y)_{k}|\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\frac{|k|}{q_{j}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\pm\{q_{j},2q_{j}\}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi(a_{j}-\frac{1}{q_{j}})|k|}\,|g_{q_{j}}|_{a_{j},b_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}|g_{q_{j}}|_{a_{j},b_{j}}\;\lesssim\;\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\Big|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big|^{2},

where in the last inequality we used (
6.5
).
Combining this with (
8.14
) and (
8.12
), and noting that

\sup_{\Im x=0}|(g_{q_{j}}-\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}})(x,y)|\;\leq\;|g_{q_{j}}-\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}|_{1/q_{j},b_{j}},

we obtain

\sup_{|y|<b_{j}}|g_{q_{j}}^{*}(y)|\;\lesssim\;\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\Big|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big|^{2}.

It follows from
\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}=g_{q_{j}}^{*}+\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}^{\bullet}
that

\bigl|\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}\bigr|_{1/q_{j},b_{j}}\;\lesssim\;\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q_{j}}\Big|\omega-\frac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\Big|^{2}.

(8.16)

Almost equidistributed orbits.

We now show that
F_{q_{j}}
has two geometrically distinct
(p_{j},q_{j})
-periodic orbits and they are
almost equidistributed
in the sense that they closely shadow the sequence of points
(x^{*}+kp_{j}/q_{j},0)
,
k=0,1,\ldots,q_{j}-1
for some
x^{*}\in\mathbb{T}_{1/q_{j}}
.

To ease the notation, we will suppress the subscripts
j
and
q_{j}
in the following two paragraphs and write simply
(p,q)=(p_{j},q_{j})
,
N=N_{j}
,
\sigma=\sigma_{j}
,
g=g_{q_{j}}
,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{q_{j}}
,
a=a_{j}=1-3/q
and
b=b_{j}=|\omega-p/q|/5
.
We will apply the estimates (
8.12
), (
8.13
), (
8.15
) and (
8.16
) obtained by the preceding paragraph.
Also recall that, by (
8.11
),

\mathfrak{g}(x,0)=\frac{\sigma\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}}{2\pi q}\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega\Big)^{2}\sin(2\pi qx)+g^{*}(0).

The first term on the right hand side has exactly
2q
simple zeros at
x=k/(2q)
for
k=0,\ldots,2q-1
.
By (
8.15
), we may view the second term
g^{*}(0)
as a small perturbation of the first term and use Rouché’s theorem and periodicity of
\mathfrak{g}
to conclude that
\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)
has exactly
2q
simple zeros in
\mathbb{T}_{1/q}
which are equidistributed and close to the points
k/(2q)
.
More precisely, for some
x^{*}\in\mathbb{T}_{a}
with
|x^{*}|\lesssim q^{\nu_{0}-\nu-1}
, we have

\mathfrak{g}\Big(x^{*}_{\pm}+\frac{k}{q},0\Big)=0,\quad\text{where $k=0,\ldots,q-1\ $, $x^{*}_{+}=x^{*}$ and $x^{*}_{-}=\frac{1}{2q}-x^{*}$.}

(8.17)

By
1/q
-periodicity of
\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)
, it is readily verified that the points
(x^{*}_{\pm}+k/q,0)
are
(p,q)
-periodic under the ‘reduced’ map

\mathfrak{F}_{q}(x,y)=(x+p/q+y,y+\mathfrak{g}(x,y)).

(8.18)

In view of (
8.12
), we may consider the map

F_{q}(x,y)=(x+p/q+y,y+g(x,y))

as a small perturbation of (
8.18
).
We will show in the following lemma that the periodic orbits of
\mathfrak{F}_{q}
starting from
(x^{*}_{\pm}+k/q,0)
persist under this perturbation.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
x_{\pm}^{*}+k/q
be the zeros of
\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)
given by
(
8.17
).
Set

a^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{2q},\qquad b^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{6}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|,\qquad\alpha_{\pm}^{k}:=\frac{\partial\mathfrak{g}}{\partial x}\Bigl(x_{\pm}^{*}+\frac{k}{q},0\Bigr).

Then, for
q
sufficiently large (depending only on the universal implicit
constants in the estimates below), the following holds.

There exist exactly
2q
distinct points

(\widetilde{x}_{+}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{+}^{k}),\qquad(\widetilde{x}_{-}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{-}^{k}),\qquad k=0,\dots,q-1,

lying in
\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}}
which are
(p,q)
-periodic under the map
F_{q}
.
More precisely, these
2q
points form exactly two geometrically distinct
(p,q)
-periodic orbits of
F_{q}
:
for each sign
\pm
,

F_{q}^{n}(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k})=\bigl(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k+np},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k+np}\bigr),\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z},

where the index
k+np
is taken modulo
q
.

Furthermore, the points
(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k})
depend analytically on
the parameters
(\sigma_{j})_{j}
and on
\phi_{0}
, and the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\bigl|\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k}-(x_{\pm}^{*}+k/q)\bigr|\;\lesssim\;q\,\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q},\qquad|\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k}|\;\lesssim\;q^{3}\mathrm{e}^{-6\pi q}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|^{2},

(8.19)

\displaystyle\bigl|\det\bigl(\mathrm{d}_{(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k})}F_{q}^{q}-\id\bigr)+q^{2}\alpha_{\pm}^{k}\bigr|\;\ll\;q^{2}|\alpha_{\pm}^{k}|.

(8.20)

Remark
.

In the above lemma and its proof, we use the notation
A\ll B
to mean that for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
Q>0
depending only on
\varepsilon
such that
|A/B|<\varepsilon
for all
q\geq Q
.

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

Consider the interpolation map

F^{\tau}_{q}(x,y)=(x+p/q+y,y+\mathfrak{g}(x,y)+\tau(g(x,y)-\mathfrak{g}(x,y))).

Since
b\ll q^{-2}
and
(3q-1)(|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}+|g-\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b})\ll b
by (
8.16
) and (
8.12
), we may apply Lemma
B.2
(with
f=0
and
a=1/q
) to
F^{\tau}_{q}
for
|\tau|\leq R
with some
R>1
to obtain
\gamma^{\tau},\Gamma^{\tau}:\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{C}
depending analytically on
\tau
.
Using Cauchy’s estimate in
\tau
and choosing
R
such that, for example,
(3q-1)(|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}+R|\mathfrak{g}-g|_{1/q,b})=b
, we have

\left|{\Gamma^{1}-\Gamma^{0}}\right|_{a^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\sup_{|\tau|\leq R}|\Gamma^{\tau}|_{a^{\prime}}}{R-1}\lesssim q|\mathfrak{g}-g|_{1/q,b}.

(8.21)

We first consider
k=0
and look for the periodic orbit near the point
(x^{*}_{+},0)
.
The cases for
x^{*}_{-}
and other
k
can be treated similarly.
Observe in particular that by the explicit formula (
8.11
) for
\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)
and the equality
x^{*}_{-}=(2q)^{-1}-x^{*}_{+}
, the quantity
\alpha^{k}_{\pm}
is independent of
k
and
\alpha^{0}_{+}=-\alpha^{0}_{-}
.

From now on until the end of the proof, we write
x^{*}=x^{*}_{+}
for simplicity of notation.
Let us put
\alpha=\alpha^{0}_{+}=\partial\mathfrak{g}/\partial x(x^{*},0)
.
By the explicit formula (
8.11
),

\alpha=\sigma\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega\Big)^{2}\cos(2\pi qx^{*}).

(8.22)

Since
(x^{*},0)
is
(p,q)
-periodic under
\mathfrak{F}_{q}
, it follows from Lemma
B.2
that
\Gamma^{0}(x^{*})=0
and
\gamma^{0}(x^{*})=0
.
By (
B.8
) and periodicity of
\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)
as well as the estimates (
8.16
) and
b\ll q^{-2}

\left|{\Gamma^{0}-q\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)}\right|_{a^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{q^{2}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}^{2}}{b}.

(8.23)

By Cauchy’s estimate and
|x^{*}|\lesssim q^{\nu_{0}-\nu-1}\ll a^{\prime}
, we have

\left|{\dot{\Gamma}^{0}(x^{*})-q\alpha}\right|\lesssim\frac{q^{3}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}^{2}}{b}\ll q|\alpha|.

(8.24)

The last
\ll
can be obtained from the explicit (
8.22
).
We apply Lemma
8.4
with
R=a^{\prime}-|x^{*}|
,
\eta_{0}=\Gamma^{0}
and
\eta_{1}=\Gamma^{1}-\Gamma^{0}
.
To verify the condition (
8.29
), observe that, by (
8.21
), (
8.12
) and (
8.16
),

r_{0}r_{1}\leq\frac{|\Gamma^{0}|_{a^{\prime}}|\Gamma^{0}-\Gamma^{1}|_{a^{\prime}}}{(a^{\prime}-|x^{*}|)^{2}}\lesssim q^{4}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}|g-\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}\ll q^{2}|\alpha|^{2}.

Combining with (
8.24
), we readily verify the condition (
8.29
) for
q
sufficiently large.
Hence, the Lemma applies and we obtain a zero point
\widetilde{x}
of
\Gamma^{1}
satisfying

|\widetilde{x}-x^{*}|\lesssim\frac{|\Gamma^{1}-\Gamma^{0}|_{a^{\prime}}}{|\dot{\Gamma}^{0}(x^{*})|}\lesssim\frac{|\mathfrak{g}-g|_{1/q,b}}{|\alpha|}\lesssim q\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q}.

(8.25)

Let us put
\widetilde{y}:=\gamma^{1}(\widetilde{x})
.
Then, since
\gamma^{0}(x^{*})=0
, by (
8.21
), (
8.25
) and the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle|\widetilde{y}|\leq

\displaystyle|\gamma^{1}(\widetilde{x})-\gamma^{0}(\widetilde{x})|+|\gamma^{0}(\widetilde{x})-\gamma^{0}(x^{*})|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle q|\mathfrak{g}-g|_{1/q,b}+\frac{q^{2}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}|\mathfrak{g}-g|_{1/q,b}}{|\alpha|}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{q^{2}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}|\mathfrak{g}-g|_{1/q,b}}{|\alpha|}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle q^{3}\mathrm{e}^{-6\pi q}|\omega-p/q|^{2}.

In the second last line, we have used
|\alpha|\lesssim q|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}
, which follows from Cauchy’s estimate.
We have established the estimates (
8.19
) for
k=0
.

For (
8.20
), we decompose

\displaystyle|\det(\mathrm{d}_{(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})}F_{q}^{q}-\id)+q^{2}\alpha|

(8.26)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\det(\mathrm{d}_{(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})}F_{q}^{q}-\id)+q\dot{\Gamma}^{1}(\widetilde{x})|+|q\dot{\Gamma}^{1}(\widetilde{x})-q\dot{\Gamma}^{0}(x^{*})|+|q\dot{\Gamma}^{0}(x^{*})-q^{2}\alpha|.

By (
8.24
), the last term on the right hand side is
\ll q^{2}|\alpha|
.
By the second inequality of (
8.30
), the second term on the right hand side of (
8.26
) can be estimated as

|q\dot{\Gamma}^{1}(\widetilde{x})-q\dot{\Gamma}^{0}(x^{*})|\lesssim\frac{q^{4}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}|g-\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}}{|\alpha|}\ll q^{2}|\alpha|.

Finally, by (
B.9
), the first term on the right hand side of (
8.26
) can be estimated as

|\det(\mathrm{d}_{(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})}F_{q}^{q}-\id)+q\dot{\Gamma}^{1}(\widetilde{x})|\lesssim\frac{q^{4}|g|_{1/q,b}^{2}}{b}\ll q^{2}|\alpha|.

This completes the proof of (
8.20
) for
(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})=(\widetilde{x}^{0}_{+},\widetilde{y}^{0}_{+})
.
The cases for other
k
can be treated similarly by replacing
x^{*}
with
x^{*}_{\pm}+k/q
in the above argument.

Finally, we show the uniqueness of the
(p,q)
-periodic points in
\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}}
, where
b^{\prime}=|\omega-p/q|/6
as in the statement.
Since
q^{-4}\ll|\alpha|/(q^{2}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b})\lesssim|\dot{\Gamma}^{0}(x^{*})|(a^{\prime}-|x^{*}|)^{2}/|\Gamma^{0}|_{a^{\prime}}
, Lemma
8.4
implies that
\widetilde{x}^{k}_{\pm}
is the unique zero of
\Gamma^{1}
in the
q^{-4}
-neighborhood of
x^{*}_{\pm}+k/q
.
Hence, any
(p,q)
-periodic point of
F_{q}
lying in a
q^{-4}
-neighborhood of some
x^{*}_{\pm}+k/q
in the
x
-coordinate must coincide with the point
(\widetilde{x}^{k}_{\pm},\widetilde{y}^{k}_{\pm})
constructed above.
We need to show that there is no
(p,q)
-periodic point in
\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}}
which lies outside these neighborhoods.
By the explicit expression for
\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)
,

q^{-6}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}|\omega-p/q|^{2}\ll|\mathfrak{g}(x,0)|

(8.27)

for
x\in\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}
lying outside the union of the
q^{-4}
-neighborhoods of the points
x^{*}_{\pm}+k/q
.
On the other hand, by (
8.23
) and (
8.21
), we have

\left|{\Gamma^{1}-q\mathfrak{g}(\cdot,0)}\right|_{a^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{q^{2}|\mathfrak{g}|_{1/q,b}^{2}}{b}+q|\mathfrak{g}-g|_{1/q,b}\lesssim q^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q}|\omega-p/q|^{3}+q\mathrm{e}^{-6\pi q}|\omega-p/q|^{2}.

(8.28)

Combining (
8.27
) and (
8.28
), we deduce that
\Gamma^{1}
does not vanish outside the
q^{-4}
-neighborhoods of the points
x^{*}_{\pm}+k/q
, which implies via Lemma
B.2
that the points
(\widetilde{x}^{k}_{\pm},\widetilde{y}^{k}_{\pm})
constructed above are the only
(p,q)
-periodic points of
F_{q}
in
\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}}
.
It is also simple to verify, for example using the formula for
F_{q}
and
|\widetilde{y}^{k}_{\pm}|\ll q^{-4}
, that we must have
F_{q}^{n}(\widetilde{x}^{k}_{\pm},\widetilde{y}^{k}_{\pm})=(\widetilde{x}^{k+np}_{\pm},\widetilde{y}^{k+np}_{\pm})
.
Finally, the analyticity of the points
(\widetilde{x}^{k}_{\pm},\widetilde{y}^{k}_{\pm})
in terms of
g
follows from the construction using Lemma
8.4
and Lemma
B.2
.
The proof is complete.
∎

The following lemma was used in the proof of Lemma
8.3
. This is an elementary result in complex analysis, but we nevertheless give a proof for clarity.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
R>0
and
\eta_{0},\eta_{1}:\mathbb{D}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}
be bounded analytic functions such that
\eta_{0}(0)=0
and
\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
.
Let us denote
r_{j}=R^{-1}\sup\{|\eta_{j}(z)|:|z|<R\}
for
j=0,1
.
Suppose

r_{0}r_{1}<c|\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)|^{2}

(8.29)

for some universal constant
c
.
Then there exists a unique zero point
x
of
\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}
within the open disc
\mathbb{D}_{\bar{R}}
with

\bar{R}=2c\frac{|\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)|}{r_{0}}R.

Moreover, we have

\frac{|x|}{R}\lesssim\frac{r_{1}}{|\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)|}\quad\text{and}\quad|\eta_{0}^{\prime}(x)+\eta_{1}^{\prime}(x)-\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)|\lesssim\frac{r_{0}r_{1}}{|\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)|}.

(8.30)

Proof.

After replacing
\eta_{j}(x)
by
\eta_{j}(Rx)/(R\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0))
we may assume
R=1
and
\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)=1
so that
r_{j}
is the uniform norm of
\eta_{j}
over the unit disc.
By the mean value theorem we can write

\eta_{0}(x)=x+x^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\eta_{0}^{\prime\prime}(tx)(1-t)dt.

By Cauchy’s estimate applied to
|x|<1/2
, we have
|\eta_{0}(x)-x|<|x|/2
if
|x|<(16r_{0})^{-1}
.
Hence, if
r_{1}<(32r_{0})^{-1}
, then Rouché’s theorem implies that there exists a unique simple zero
x^{*}
of
\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}
in the disc
|x|<(16r_{0})^{-1}
.
In fact, Rouché’s theorem applies to the circle of radius
2r_{1}
and we deduce
|x^{*}|\lesssim r_{1}
.
By the mean value theorem and Cauchy’s estimate, we have
|\eta_{0}^{\prime}(x^{*})+\eta_{1}^{\prime}(x^{*})-\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)|\lesssim r_{0}|x^{*}|+r_{1}\lesssim r_{0}r_{1}
.
In the last
\lesssim
we used the fact
r_{0}\geq 1
, which follows from Cauchy’s estimate of
|\eta_{0}^{\prime}(0)|
.
After scaling
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
back to the original functions, we obtain the desired conclusions with
c=1/32
.
∎

We complement Lemma
8.3
with the following corollary showing the uniqueness of the orbits that it constructs.

Corollary 8.5
.

Suppose that
\phi
is real-valued, so that the standard map
F
acts on
the real locus
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
in the adapted coordinates.
Then the
(p,q)
-periodic points
(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k})
of
F_{q}

obtained in Lemma
8.3
lie in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, and these are the only

(p,q)
-periodic orbits of
F
contained in the real locus.

Proof of Corollary
8.5
.

Let
\phi
be real-valued so that
F
acts on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
.
By Lemma
8.3
, the points
(\widetilde{x}^{k}_{\pm},\widetilde{y}^{k}_{\pm})_{k=0}^{q-1}
in Lemma
8.3
exhaust all
(p,q)
-periodic points in the domain
\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}}
of
F_{q}
.
To prove the required statement it is enough to show that all
(p,q)
-orbits of
F
must intersect the subset
H_{q}(\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}})
.
For this, it suffices to show that every
(p,q)
-periodic orbit of
F
(in the real locus
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
) must intersect the real annulus
A
defined as

A:=\mathbb{T}\times\bigg\{y\in\mathbb{R}\ :\ \bigg|y-\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega\Big)\bigg|<\frac{1}{15}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\bigg\}.

Indeed, the estimate (
6.3
) clearly implies
A\subset H_{q}(\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}})
.

We also recall that the conditions
\|\dddot{\phi}\|\leq 1
and
\phi^{*}=0
imposed by Theorem
6.1
automatically guarantee that
\|\dot{\phi}\|\leq(2\pi)^{-2}
by definition of the
\|\cdot\|
-norm.
Then, by the mean value theorem applied to (
5.6
) and the bound on
\|\dot{\phi}\|
, it is straightforward to show (for example using the formula (
9.50
)) that
\max(2|f(x,y)|,|g(x,y)|)<|y|/8
.

Now, let
(x_{k},y_{k})=F^{k}(x_{0},y_{0})
be a
(p,q)
-periodic orbit under
F
lifted to the universal cover. In particular,
x_{q}=x_{0}+p
and
y_{q}=y_{0}
.
Assume for a contradiction that
(x_{k},y_{k})
does not intersect
A
for any
k
.
Then we observe that
y_{k}-(p/q-\omega)
must have the same sign for all
k
.
For, if not, then we have
|y_{k}-y_{k+1}|\geq\frac{2}{15}|\omega-p/q|
and
|y_{k}|\leq\frac{14}{15}|\omega-p/q|
for some
k
.
By (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), we deduce
\frac{2}{15}|\omega-p/q|\leq|g(x_{k},y_{k})|
.
However, this implies
\frac{2}{15}|\omega-p/q|<|y_{k}|/8
, which contradicts
|y_{k}|<|\omega-p/q|
.

We have shown that all points
(x_{k},y_{k})
of the orbit lie on the same side of
A
.
Suppose for example
p/q-\omega>0
and
y_{k}\geq\frac{16}{15}(p/q-\omega)
for all
k
.
Since
x_{q}-x_{0}=p
, we have
x_{k+1}-x_{k}\leq p/q
for some
k
, which implies
y_{k}+f(x_{k},y_{k})\leq p/q-\omega
.
On the other hand, since
|f(x_{k},y_{k})|<|y_{k}|/16
we have
y_{k}<(p/q-\omega)/(1-1/16)
, which contradicts
y_{k}>\frac{16}{15}(p/q-\omega)
.
Hence, we have shown that any
(p,q)
-orbit of
F
must intersect
A
, which by
A\subset H_{q}(\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}})
implies that it intersects
H_{q}(\mathbb{T}_{a^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}_{b^{\prime}})
.
The cases
y_{k}\leq\frac{14}{15}(p/q-\omega)
for all
k
and the cases with
p/q-\omega<0
can be treated similarly.
It follows from this argument that this orbit must intersect
A
and it must be one of the
(p,q)
-orbits obtained by Lemma
8.3
.
∎

End of proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Finally, we show that for each
j
the two periodic orbits constructed in Lemma
8.3
satisfy the conclusions of Lemma
8.1
, thereby completing its proof.

The uniqueness statement in part (1) of Lemma
8.1
follows immediately from Corollary
8.5
.
We now verify the estimates (
8.5
) and (
8.3
) for these periodic orbits.

We begin with (
8.5
).
In the case of the zero
x_{-}^{*}+k/q
, the corresponding quantity
\alpha_{-}^{k}
in (
8.20
) is given by

\alpha_{-}^{k}=-\sigma\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}\Bigl(\frac{p}{q}-\omega\Bigr)^{2}\cos(2\pi qx^{*}).

Thus,

\left|{\frac{\det\bigl(\mathrm{d}_{(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k})}F_{q}^{q}-\id\bigr)}{q^{2}(p/q-\omega)^{2}}\pm\sigma\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}\cos(2\pi qx^{*})}\right|\;\ll\;|\sigma|\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

From the estimate
|x^{*}|\lesssim q^{\nu_{0}-\nu-1}\ll 1/q
we obtain

|\cos(2\pi qx^{*})-1|\ll 1
.
The desired estimate (
8.5
) now follows from the definition of

\Lambda_{j,\pm}
by taking
q
sufficiently large.

We next turn to (
8.3
).
Recall that, by (
6.10
), the function
S_{q}
satisfies

\biggl|S_{q}(x,y)-\Bigl(\frac{p}{q}-\omega+y\Bigr)^{2}\frac{1+f_{1}(x)}{2\bigl(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}(x)\bigr)^{2}}\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|^{3},

(8.31)

with
\nu_{0}=64
.
Substituting
x=\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k}
,
y=\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k}
and using the mean value theorem
together with (
8.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\biggl|\Bigl(\frac{p}{q}-\omega+\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k}\Bigr)^{2}\frac{1+f_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k})}{2\bigl(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k})\bigr)^{2}}-\Bigl(\frac{p}{q}-\omega\Bigr)^{2}\frac{1+f_{1}(x_{\pm}^{*}+k/q)}{2\bigl(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}(x_{\pm}^{*}+k/q)\bigr)^{2}}\biggr|

(8.32)

\displaystyle\lesssim\;\biggl\|\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{2}}\biggr\|\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|\,|\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k}|+\biggl\|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Bigl(\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{2}}\Bigr)\biggr\|\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|^{2}\biggl|\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k}-\Bigl(x_{\pm}^{*}+\frac{k}{q}\Bigr)\biggr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\;\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|\,|\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k}|+\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|^{2}\biggl|\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k}-\Bigl(x_{\pm}^{*}+\frac{k}{q}\Bigr)\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|^{2}.

In the last line we have used the basic estimates

\|f_{1}\|<1/2
and
\|\dot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim\|\ddot{\phi}\|
, which follow from the definition (
6.8
).

Summing over
k=0,\dots,q-1
yields

\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\frac{1+f_{1}(x_{\pm}^{*}+k/q)}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}(x_{\pm}^{*}+k/q))^{2}}=q\biggl\langle\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{2}}\biggr\rangle_{q}(x_{\pm}^{*})=\frac{q}{2\bigl(1+f_{1}^{*}+\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}(x_{\pm}^{*})\bigr)}.

(8.33)

Recall that
x_{+}^{*}=x^{*}
and
x_{-}^{*}=(2q)^{-1}-x^{*}
with

|x^{*}|\lesssim q^{\nu_{0}-\nu-1}\ll 1/q
.
In particular, we have
|\Im x^{*}_{\pm}|\ll 1
.
By Cauchy’s estimate and the mean value theorem we obtain

\bigl|\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}(x_{+}^{*})-\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}(0)\bigr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}|\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}|_{1/q}\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k|\geq q\\
q\mid k\end{subarray}}|f_{1}|\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi(1-1/q)|k|}\biggr)\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

(8.34)

The second
\lesssim
follows by estimating the Fourier coefficients of
f_{1}
individually using analyticity over
\mathbb{T}_{1}
.
Similarly

\biggl|\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}(x_{-}^{*})-\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2q}\Bigr)\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

(8.35)

Moreover, for
\Im x=0
we have

|\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}^{>q}(x)|\leq\sum_{|k|>q,\;q\mid k}|f_{1}|\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi|k|}\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q}
.
Combining this with (
8.34
) and (
8.35
) gives

\biggl|\pm\bigl((\hat{f}_{1})_{q}+(\hat{f}_{1})_{-q}\bigr)-\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}(x_{\pm}^{*})\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q},

where
(\hat{f}_{1})_{\pm q}
are the Fourier coefficients of
f_{1}
of order
\pm q
.
It follows that

\biggl|\frac{1}{1+f_{1}^{*}\pm((\hat{f}_{1})_{q}+(\hat{f}_{1})_{-q})}-\frac{1}{1+f_{1}^{*}+\langle f_{1}^{\bullet}\rangle_{q}(x_{\pm}^{*})}\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

(8.36)

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
denote the rescaled action defined by the right-hand side of
(
8.2
) with
\xi_{j}
taken to be the
(p,q)
-orbit of

(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k})
under
F_{q}
, i.e.

\mathcal{A}_{\pm}:=\frac{1}{q(\omega-p/q)^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}S_{q}(\widetilde{x}_{\pm}^{k},\widetilde{y}_{\pm}^{k}).

Combining (
8.31
), (
8.32
),
(
8.33
), and (
8.36
) yields

\biggl|\mathcal{A}_{\pm}-\frac{1}{2\bigl(1+f_{1}^{*}\pm((\hat{f}_{1})_{q}+(\hat{f}_{1})_{-q})\bigr)}\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|+q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

Since
|(\hat{f}_{1})_{\pm q}|\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}
, up to an additional error

\lesssim q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}
we may write

\biggl|\mathcal{A}_{\pm}-\frac{1}{2(1+f_{1}^{*})}\biggl(1\mp\frac{(\hat{f}_{1})_{q}+(\hat{f}_{1})_{-q}}{1+f_{1}^{*}}\biggr)\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|+q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

(8.37)

We now control
(\hat{f}_{1})_{q}+(\hat{f}_{1})_{-q}
via the choice of the coefficients

\sigma_{j}
in (
8.9
).
Recall from (
8.13
) and the definition of
\mathfrak{g}
that

\Bigl\|\mathfrak{g}^{\bullet}(\cdot,0)+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\frac{p}{q}-\omega\Bigr)^{2}\biggl\langle\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}}\biggr\rangle_{q}^{N}\Bigr\|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|^{2}.

Since
N=2q
, the
k
th Fourier coefficient of the expression inside the

\|\cdot\|
-norm is nonzero only for
|k|=q
or
2q
, and analyticity in
\mathbb{T}_{1}

gives a bound
q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}|\omega-p/q|^{2}
for such coefficients.
Hence the supremum norm over the real torus
\mathbb{T}
is bounded by

\lesssim q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}|\omega-p/q|^{2}
.
A similar argument shows that the supremum norm over
\mathbb{T}
of

\langle\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{-1}\rangle^{>N}

is bounded by
\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-6\pi q}
.
For
\Im x=0
, the explicit formula (
8.11
) then yields

\biggl|\frac{\sigma\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}}{2\pi q}\sin(2\pi qx)+\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}(x)}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}(x)}\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

(8.38)

We decompose

\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}=f_{1}^{*}+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}^{q}+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}^{>q}
.
Analyticity and
\Im x=0
imply

|\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}^{>q}(x)|\lesssim q\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q}
,

|\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}^{q}|\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}
,
and
|\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}^{q}|\lesssim q\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}
, hence

\biggl|\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}(x)}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}(x)}-\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}^{q}(x)}{1+f_{1}^{*}}\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q\mathrm{e}^{-4\pi q}\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

Substituting this into (
8.38
) and comparing the Fourier coefficients
of order
\pm q
, we obtain

\biggl|\frac{\sigma\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}}{(2\pi q)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(\hat{f}_{1})_{q}+(\hat{f}_{1})_{-q}}{1+f_{1}^{*}}\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

Combining this with (
8.37
), we arrive at

\biggl|\mathcal{A}_{\pm}-\frac{1}{2(1+f_{1}^{*})}\Bigl(1\mp\frac{2\sigma\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}}{(2\pi q)^{2}}\Bigr)\biggr|\;\lesssim\;q^{\nu_{0}}\biggl|\omega-\frac{p}{q}\biggr|+q^{\nu_{0}-\nu}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}.

This is precisely (
8.3
).
The proof of Lemma
8.1
is complete.

We have now completed all steps in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, with the sole exception of the technical intermediate result Theorem
6.1
.
The remainder of the article will be devoted to the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

9
Proof of Theorem
6.1

Before entering the proof of Theorem
6.1
, we broadly outline the main steps.

We start with the standard map (
5.5
) in adapted coordinates, where the RIC is given by
\{y=0\}
.
For a fixed pair
(p,q)
, we focus on a complex neighbourhood of the line
\{y=p/q-\omega\}
.
On this neighbourhood, we construct two successive changes of coordinates, denoted by
\Theta
and
\id+\Sigma
, in Section
9.1
and Section
9.2
, respectively.

The common idea behind the construction of these two coordinate changes is to consider a near-identity change of variables

\Phi=\id+\Psi\quad\text{where}\quad\Psi(x,y)=(\psi(x,y),\varphi(x,y))

(9.1)

for some functions
\psi
and
\varphi
.
Put
A(x,y)=(x+\omega+y,y)
and
G(x,y)=(f(x,y),g(x,y))
.
Let

\Phi\circ F\circ\Phi^{-1}=\widetilde{F}=A+\widetilde{G}

where
\widetilde{G}(x,y)=(\widetilde{f}(x,y),\widetilde{g}(x,y))
.
Expanding
\widetilde{F}\circ\Phi=\Phi\circ F
, we have

\widetilde{G}\circ(\id+\Psi)=G+\Psi\circ F+A-A\circ(\id+\Psi).

Componentwise, this is

\begin{cases}\widetilde{f}\circ(\id+\Psi)=f+\psi\circ F-\psi-\varphi,\\
\widetilde{g}\circ(\id+\Psi)=g+\varphi\circ F-\varphi.\end{cases}

(9.2)

To simplify
\widetilde{F}
as much as possible, we shall make
\widetilde{f}=0
by fixing

\varphi=f+\psi\circ F-\psi.

(9.3)

Then
\widetilde{F}(x,y)=(x+p/q+y,y+\widetilde{g}(x,y))
with

\widetilde{g}\circ(\id+\Psi)=g+(f\circ F-f)+(\psi\circ F^{2}-2\psi\circ F+\psi).

(9.4)

Our goal is to choose
\psi
so that
\widetilde{g}(\cdot,0)
is closely approximated by its resonant part
\langle g_{q}\rangle_{q}^{N}
, as formalized by the estimate (
6.6
).

In the first step, we base the construction of
\psi
on a formal Taylor-series computation, and the coordinate transform (
9.1
) is accordingly defined over a neighbourhood of
\{y=0\}
which contains the circle
\{y=p/q-\omega\}
.
More precisely, we start in Section
9.1.1
by analyzing the Taylor expansion

\widetilde{g}(x,y)=\widetilde{g}_{1}(x)y+\widetilde{g}_{2}(x)y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3})

(9.5)

where
\widetilde{g}
is as given in (
9.4
), and we take

\psi(x,y)=\psi_{1}(x)y+\psi_{2}(x)y^{2}

with suitable choices of
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
so that
\widetilde{g}_{1}
and
\widetilde{g}_{2}
are almost
1/q
-periodic.
The resulting coordinate transform
\Phi
will not be symplectic in general.

In order to evaluate the symplectic action of any orbit found in these new coordinates, we also compute the leading term
\widetilde{s}_{2}
in the Taylor expansion

\widetilde{S}(x,y)=\widetilde{s}_{2}(x)y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3})

(9.6)

of the pullback
\widetilde{S}=S\circ\Phi^{-1}
of the generating function
S
.

Then, we restrict everything to a neighbourhood of
\{y=p/q-\omega\}
.
This requires evaluating the Taylor expansion around
y=0
at a finite
y\approx p/q-\omega
, and hence we also need a quantitative estimate of the
\mathcal{O}(y^{3})
remainders in (
9.5
) and (
9.6
).
This estimate will be the main content of Section
9.1.3
.
The coordinate transform resulting from Step 1 will be constructed as
\Theta=\Phi^{-1}\circ\tau
where
\tau
is a translation that re-centers the domain at
y=p/q-\omega
.

Roughly speaking, at
y\approx p/q-\omega
the Taylor remainders in (
9.5
) and (
9.6
) have size
\sim|\omega-p/q|^{3}
, whereas the
q
-resonant part of
\widetilde{g}
restricted to the real torus
\mathbb{T}
has size
\sim|\omega-p/q|^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}
.
Since
\widetilde{g}
has a rather explicit dependence on
\phi
, this informally indicates that the eigendata of the
(p,q)
-orbit can be controlled by the
q\mathbb{Z}
-Fourier modes of
\phi
over a range of size at most
\sim|\omega-p/q|^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi q}
.
It can be verified, although we do not do so here, that if we impose the stronger arithmetic condition
|\omega-p/q|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi Cq}
for some
C>1
, then the size
|\omega-p/q|^{3}
of the error term will be small enough and the first coordinate change already suffices to deduce the main results.

If, however, the size of
|\omega-p/q|
is only polynomially small in
q
, as assumed in part (1) of the main theorem, then the error contributed by the Taylor remainder outweighs the size of the
q
-resonant part of
\widetilde{g}
.
Therefore, we need an iterative scheme in Step 2 to reduce the non-resonant errors further.

In Section
9.2.2
, we use a similar type of coordinate transform given by (
9.1
)–(
9.4
) but with
\omega=p/q
, since we have re-centered the coordinates at
y=p/q-\omega
in Step 1.
The coordinate transform obtained in Step 2 is near-identity and is denoted
\id+\Sigma
.
The iteration scheme is similar in spirit to the works
[
1
]
and
[
13
]
.
Unlike
[
1
]
, the coordinates we obtain are not symplectic.

We remark that, in contrast to Step 1, the construction in Step 2 (Section
9.2.2
) is nonexplicit because it involves
\sim q
iterations of coordinate transforms.
Hence, while we can make the non-resonant part (
9.69
) of
\widetilde{g}
exponentially small in
q
, we do not have a better approximation of the resonant part in (
9.70
) — we do not have sufficient information on the contribution to the resonant part from this iteration scheme.

For technical reasons, we cannot skip Step 1 and directly begin with the iteration scheme in Step 2.
This is because, without Step 1, the first iteration of the scheme in Step 2 will introduce an ‘error’ of comparable size to the resonant part of the original map, and it seems difficult to analyze this ‘error’ term explicitly.
On the other hand, Step 1 ensures that the non-resonant part of the map is small enough so that errors introduced by each iteration in Step 2 are always ‘much smaller’ than the resonant part.
This situation appears similar to Lazutkin’s proof of existence of invariant curves near the boundary of a convex billiard table: in his proof, one has to transform the original coordinates into higher-order ‘Lazutkin coordinates’ so that the non-integrable part of the billiard map vanishes to sufficiently high order near the boundary before applying the KAM iteration.

We also remark that, while the method in Step 2 yields the weaker arithmetic condition (
1.6
) for the results on Lyapunov exponents, it does not improve the condition (
1.9
) for the results on symplectic actions.
The key issue is that, whereas Lyapunov exponents can be computed in
any
coordinates, the symplectic action is invariant only under exact symplectic coordinate transforms.
Although the coordinate transform in Step 1 is non-symplectic, it is fairly explicit, and we can approximate the symplectic action using just the leading term (
9.6
),
provided
the Taylor remainder is much smaller than the leading term, as ensured by the strong condition (
1.9
).
If only weaker conditions of the type (
1.6
) are assumed, then the relevant information on the symplectic actions of
q
-periodic orbits is contained in the first
\sim q
terms in the Taylor expansion (
9.6
).
In other words, it is unclear how to analyze the dependence of the
q
-resonant part of the restricted generating function
S(\cdot,y)
on
\phi
when
|y-\omega|
is only
polynomially
small in
q
.

We now begin the proof of Theorem
6.1
proper.

9.1
Step 1: coordinate changes via Taylor expansion

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and
\phi:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic with zero average and
\|\dddot{\phi}\|\leq 1
.
Let
F

be the standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in the adapted coordinates.
Then the following statements hold.
For
q\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
sufficiently large, if

\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\leq\frac{q^{-6}}{6}\qquad\text{for some}\quad p\in\mathbb{Z},

(9.7)

then there exists an analytic change of coordinates
\Theta
on the domain
\mathbb{T}_{1-q^{-1}}\times\mathbb{D}_{|\omega-p/q|/2}
which is close to a translation by
p/q-\omega
in
y
in the sense that

\Theta(x,y)=(x+\theta_{1}(x,y),\;y+p/q-\omega+\theta_{2}(x,y))

(9.8)

with

|\theta_{1}|_{1-1/q,|\omega-p/q|/2}\lesssim q\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;\quad|\theta_{2}|_{1-1/q,|\omega-p/q|/2}<\frac{1}{11}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|,

(9.9)

and the map
\widetilde{F}_{q}:=\Theta^{-1}\circ F\circ\Theta
in these new coordinates writes

\widetilde{F}_{q}:\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x+\frac{p}{q}+y\\
y+\widetilde{g}_{q}(x,y)\end{pmatrix}.

Define
f_{1}:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
as in (
6.8
) and
\widetilde{S}_{q}:=S\circ\Theta
, where
S
is the normalized generating function associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in adapted coordinates.
Then for
\nu_{0}=64
and for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}_{1-1/q}\times\mathbb{D}_{|\omega-p/q|/2}
,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\widetilde{g}_{q}(x,y)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega+y\Big)\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega-y\Big)\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}(x)}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}(x)}\Bigg|\lesssim

\displaystyle q^{\nu_{0}}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{3}

(9.10)

\displaystyle\left|{\widetilde{S}_{q}(x,y)-\Big(\frac{p}{q}-\omega+y\Big)^{2}\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q})^{2}}(x)}\right|\lesssim

\displaystyle q^{\nu_{0}}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{3}.

Moreover, the coordinate transform
\Theta
depends analytically on
\phi
and is real-analytic when
\phi
is real-valued.
In this case, the map
\widetilde{F}_{q}
preserves the area form

\Theta^{*}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\Big(\frac{1}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}}+\widetilde{\eta}\Big)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(9.11)

for some real-analytic
\widetilde{\eta}
such that

|\widetilde{\eta}|_{1-\frac{1}{q},\frac{1}{2}|\omega-\frac{p}{q}|}\lesssim q^{\nu_{0}}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|.

(9.12)

The rest of Section
9.1
aims to prove Proposition
9.1
.

9.1.1
Preliminary Taylor series calculations

Recall that the standard map associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in adapted coordinates
F
is given by (
5.5
) - (
5.6
).
Consider a change of coordinates
\Phi=\id+\Psi
as described in (
9.1
) - (
9.4
).
We shall choose
\psi
to be of the form
\psi(x,y)=\psi_{1}(x)y+\psi_{2}(x)y^{2}
for some
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
such that in a neighbourhood of
\{y=p/q-\omega\}
the function
\widetilde{g}
in (
9.4
) is almost
1/q
-periodic in
x
.

Taylor series of
F
and the generating function.

In this paragraph we obtain the Taylor expansion at
y=0
of
f
and
g
as well as the generating function
S
up to order
\mathcal{O}(y^{2})
.
Let us begin by fixing some notations.
Denote

\displaystyle f(x,y)=f_{1}(x)y+f_{2}(x)y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3})\quad\text{and}\quad g(x,y)=g_{1}(x)y+g_{2}(x)y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3}).

We adopt similar notations for the Taylor expansions of
\psi
,
\varphi
and
\widetilde{g}
.
Then, a straightforward computation using the Taylor series of (
5.6
) yields

1+f_{1}=\frac{1}{(1+\dot{\phi})(1+\dot{\phi}^{+})};\quad f_{2}=-\frac{\ddot{\phi}^{+}}{2(1+\dot{\phi})^{2}(1+\dot{\phi}^{+})^{3}};\quad g_{1}=0;\quad g_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}.

(9.13)

We remark that the last equality
g_{2}=-\dot{f}_{1}/2
can be computed from the area-preservation property
F^{*}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y)=\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y
and
g_{1}=0
.
Next, we consider the normalized generating function
S
associated with
(\omega,\phi)
in adapted coordinates (see Section
5
).
Integrating the Taylor expansion of the second equation in (
5.12
) along
y
and using the Taylor remainder formula and the normalization condition
S(x,0)=0
, we get

S(x,y)=\frac{1+f_{1}(x)}{2}y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3}).

(9.14)

Taylor series after coordinate changes.

To express the Taylor expansion of (
9.3
) and (
9.4
) in
y
, let us define a finte-difference operator
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
by

\mathcal{D}_{\omega}u:=u\circ R_{\omega}-u\quad\text{for any}\quad u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}.

(9.15)

Also recall the notation
u^{\pm}:=u\circ R_{\pm\omega}
.
Then, Taylor expanding the right hand sides of (
9.3
) and of the second line of (
9.2
)
and substituting
g_{1}=0
,
g_{2}=-\dot{f}_{1}/2
,

\displaystyle\varphi=

\displaystyle(f_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\psi_{1})y+\Big(f_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\psi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\Big)y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3}).

(9.16)

\displaystyle g+\varphi\circ F-\varphi=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1})y+\Big(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}\Big)y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3}),

On the other hand, Taylor expanding
\widetilde{g}\circ(\id+\Psi)
at
y=0
yields

\widetilde{g}\circ(\id+\Psi)=(1+\varphi_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}y+\big((1+\varphi_{1})^{2}\widetilde{g}_{2}+\varphi_{2}\widetilde{g}_{1}+(1+\varphi_{1})\psi_{1}\dot{\widetilde{g}}_{1}\big)y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3}).

(9.17)

From the first line of (
9.16
),

\varphi_{1}=f_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\psi_{1},\quad\varphi_{2}=f_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\psi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}.

(9.18)

Equating the second line of (
9.17
) with (
9.16
),

\displaystyle(1+\varphi_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1},

\displaystyle(1+\varphi_{1})^{2}\widetilde{g}_{2}+\varphi_{2}\widetilde{g}_{1}+(1+\varphi_{1})\psi_{1}\dot{\widetilde{g}}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}.

We may differentiate the first line in
x
and multiply throughout by
\psi_{1}
to obtain
(1+\varphi_{1})\psi_{1}\dot{\widetilde{g}}_{1}+\psi_{1}\dot{\varphi}_{1}\widetilde{g}_{1}=\psi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}
, which can be used to eliminate the
(1+\varphi_{1})\psi_{1}\dot{\widetilde{g}}_{1}
term on the second line above and obtain

\displaystyle(1+\varphi_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1},

(9.19)

\displaystyle(1+\varphi_{1})^{2}\widetilde{g}_{2}+(\varphi_{2}-\psi_{1}\dot{\varphi}_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}+\psi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}.

The formulae (
9.18
) and (
9.19
) together give the dependence of the leading Taylor coefficients
\widetilde{g}_{1}
and
\widetilde{g}_{2}
of
\widetilde{g}
in terms of
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
.
Our next task is to choose
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
appropriately so that
\widetilde{g}_{1}
and
\widetilde{g}_{2}
are almost
1/q
-periodic on
\mathbb{T}
.

The choice of
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
.

From now on, let
q>1
and
p\in\mathbb{Z}
be fixed.
Analogous to (
9.15
), we define a finte-difference operator
\mathcal{D}_{p/q}
by

\mathcal{D}_{p/q}u:=u\circ R_{p/q}-u\quad\text{for any}\quad u:\mathbb{T}_{a}\to\mathbb{C}.

Recall that we denote by
\langle u\rangle_{q}
and
\left\{{u}\right\}_{q}
to be the
1/q
-periodization of
u
and the complement part respectively (see Section
4
).
Given
v:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
, there is a
unique

u:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
, denoted
\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-1}v
, such that
\mathcal{D}_{p/q}u=v
and
\langle u\rangle_{q}=0
. The function
u
can be defined via its Fourier series as

\hat{u}_{k}=\begin{cases}\frac{\hat{v}_{k}}{\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k\frac{p}{q}}-1}&\text{if $q\nmid k$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Recall that
\widetilde{g}_{1}
,
\widetilde{g}_{2}
and
\widetilde{s}_{2}
denote the lead terms of the Taylor expansions of
\widetilde{g}
and
\widetilde{S}
as in (
9.5
) and (
9.6
).
In the sequel, we shall choose
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
so as to make
\{\widetilde{g}_{1}\}
and
\{\widetilde{g}_{2}\}_{q}
as small as possible assuming
p/q
is sufficiently close to
\omega
.
Informally, we will obtain

\widetilde{g}_{1}\approx\Big(\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big)\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}},\quad\widetilde{g}_{2}\approx\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}}

(9.20)

and

\widetilde{s}_{2}\approx\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{2}}.

(9.21)

In this section, we obtain the terms on the right hand sides of ‘
\approx
’ above.
The rigorous estimates of the differences between the two sides will be left to the next section.
The guiding heuristics of the choice of
\psi
that follows is that
|\omega-p/q|
shall be regarded as a ‘small’ quantity so that
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{q}
and
(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})u
generally have ‘smaller’ orders of magnitude than
\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{q}
and
u
respectively.
In fact, to achieve (
9.20
) we take
\psi(x,y)=\psi_{1}(x)y+\psi_{2}(x)y^{2}
with

\displaystyle\psi_{1}=(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\left\{{f_{1}}\right\}_{q}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-1}\{f_{1}\}_{q};

(9.22)

\displaystyle\psi_{2}=-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\left\{{\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Big(f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}}\right\}_{q}.

By the choice of
\psi_{1}
and the first line of (
9.16
),

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}=

\displaystyle f_{1}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\psi_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{p/q}\psi_{1}

(9.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{1}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\psi_{1}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-1}\left\{{f_{1}}\right\}_{q}-\left\{{f_{1}}\right\}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})(\psi_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-1}\left\{{f_{1}}\right\}_{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})^{2}\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\left\{{f_{1}}\right\}_{q}.

Thus
\varphi_{1}=\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}
and we may consider
\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}
as small errors.
It follows from the first line of (
9.19
) that

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}+\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\{{\varphi_{1}}\right\}_{q}-\left\{{\varphi_{1}}\right\}_{q}\widetilde{g}_{1}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}

(9.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big)\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}+\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}-(\omega-\frac{p}{q})\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}+\frac{\{\varphi_{1}^{+}\}_{q}-(1+\widetilde{g}_{1})\left\{{\varphi_{1}}\right\}_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}.

On the other hand, substituting (
9.18
) into the right hand side of the second line of (
9.19
) and using the choice of
\psi_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}\psi_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Big(f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}

(9.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{2})\psi_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}}\right\rangle_{q}+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{2})\psi_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}}\right\rangle_{q}+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1})\dot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{2})\psi_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}}\right\rangle_{q}+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}-\frac{1}{2}(1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q})\dot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}-\frac{1}{2}\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\dot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q})\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{2})\psi_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}-\frac{1}{2}\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\dot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}.

In the last line, we used the simple observation that
\langle(1+f_{1})\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}\rangle_{q}=(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}
.
Using
(1+\varphi_{1})^{2}=(1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q})^{2}+(2+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\varphi_{1})\{\varphi_{1}\}
, the left hand side of the second line of (
9.19
) writes

\displaystyle(1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q})^{2}\widetilde{g}_{2}+(2+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\varphi_{1})\{\varphi_{1}\}\widetilde{g}_{2}+(\varphi_{2}-\psi_{1}\dot{\varphi}_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}+\psi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}.

(9.26)

Equating (
9.26
) with (
9.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q})^{2}}\bigg[(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{2})\psi_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}}\right\rangle_{q}

(9.27)

\displaystyle+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}-\frac{1}{2}\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\dot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle-(2+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\varphi_{1})\{\varphi_{1}\}\widetilde{g}_{2}-(\varphi_{2}-\psi_{1}\dot{\varphi}_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}-\psi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}\bigg].

We now compute an approximation of the pullback
\widetilde{S}=S\circ\Phi^{-1}
of the generating function to the new coordinates using (
9.14
):

\widetilde{S}(x,y)=\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\varphi_{1})^{2}}y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3}).

(9.28)

Using
(1+\varphi_{1})^{2}=(1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q})^{2}+(2+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\varphi_{1})\{\varphi_{1}\}
, we may write

\widetilde{s}_{2}=\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{2}}-\frac{(1+f_{1})(2+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}+\varphi_{1})\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{2}(1+\varphi_{1})^{2}}.

(9.29)

9.1.2
Quantitative estimates: the leading terms of
\widetilde{g}
.

The goal of this section is to give a precise justification of the approximations (
9.20
) and (
9.21
) by showing that all but the first terms on the right hand sides of (
9.24
), (
9.27
) and (
9.29
) are relatively small.
More precisely, we prove

Lemma 9.2
.

Suppose

\|\dddot{\phi}\|\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{q^{3}}.

(9.30)

Then

\left\|\widetilde{g}_{1}-\Big(\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big)\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}\right\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{2},\qquad\left\|\widetilde{g}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}}\right\|\lesssim q^{4}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|

(9.31)

and

\left\|\widetilde{s}_{2}-\frac{1+f_{1}}{2(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{2}}\right\|\lesssim\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|.

(9.32)

Before proceeding to the proof of Lemma
9.2
,
let us note some elementary facts.
Since

k\nmid q\quad\implies\quad|\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kp/q}-1|\geq|\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/q}-1|=2\sin\frac{\pi}{q}\geq\frac{4}{q},

we have, for
u:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
analytic, that

\|\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-1}\{u\}_{q}\|=\sum_{q\nmid k}\left|{\frac{\hat{u}_{k}}{\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kp/q}-1}}\right|\mathrm{e}^{2\pi|k|}\leq\frac{q}{4}\|u\|

(9.33)

where
\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-1}\{u\}_{q}
is uniquely defined as the function
v
such that
\langle v\rangle_{q}=0
and
\mathcal{D}_{p/q}v=u
.
On the other hand, since
(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})u=u(\cdot+\omega)-u(\cdot+p/q)=\mathcal{D}_{\omega-p/q}(u\circ R_{p/q})
, where
R_{p/q}:x\mapsto x+p/q
is a rotation on
\mathbb{T}_{1}
, by Lemma
C.2
with
\delta=\omega-p/q
,

\|(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})u\|=\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega-p/q}(u\circ R_{p/q})\|\leq\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|\dot{u}\|.

(9.34)

Finally, we also remark that, after integrating the Fourier series of
\dddot{\phi}
and using the definition of
\|\;\cdot\;\|
-norm, the condition
\|\dddot{\phi}\|\leq 1
implies the following convenient estimates.

\|\ddot{\phi}\|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\dot{\phi}\|\leq\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

(9.35)

This observation has already been used previously in the proof of Corollary
8.5
.

Let us begin to prove Lemma
9.2
.

By (
9.35
) and the explicit expression (
9.13
), we first have

\|f_{1}\|\leq\frac{(2+\|\dot{\phi}\|)\|\dot{\phi}\|}{(1-\|\dot{\phi}\|)^{2}}<\frac{1}{18}.

(9.36)

In the above, we used the inequality
\|(1+f_{1})^{-1}\|\leq(1-\|f_{1}\|)^{-1}
, which follows from the fact that the space of analytic functions
\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
with finite
\|\;\cdot\;\|
-norm is a Banach algebra (Lemma
C.1
).

Furthermore, it is straightforward to deduce from
\|\dot{\phi}\|\leq(2\pi)^{-2}
and the explicit expressions (
9.13
) that

\|\dot{f}_{1}\|,\,\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|,\,\|f_{2}\|,\,\|\dot{f}_{2}\|,\,\|g_{2}\|,\,\|\dot{g}_{2}\|\,\lesssim\,1.

(9.37)

Recall that by (
9.23
) we have
\varphi_{1}=\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})^{2}\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\left\{{f_{1}}\right\}_{q}
.
Hence, by (
9.33
) - (
9.37
) and assumption
9.30
,

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{1}\|\leq

\displaystyle\|\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}\|+\|(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})^{2}\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\left\{{f_{1}}\right\}_{q}\|

(9.38)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f_{1}\|+\Big(\frac{q}{4}\Big)^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\dot{f}_{1}\|<\frac{1}{18}

for
q
sufficiently large.
Similarly, using (
9.37
)

\displaystyle\|\dot{\varphi}_{1}\|=

\displaystyle\|\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})^{2}\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\{\dot{f}_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim\|\dot{f}_{1}\|+q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim 1;

(9.39)

\displaystyle\|\ddot{\varphi}_{1}\|=

\displaystyle\|\langle\ddot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}+(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})^{2}\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\{\ddot{f}_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|+q^{2}\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}.

Remark
.

We give more precise bounds in (
9.36
) and (
9.38
) in stead of just an implicit universal constant
\lesssim 1
as in (
9.37
) and (
9.39
).
The reason is that we will need to provide lower bounds on the denominators is (
9.24
), (
9.27
) and (
9.29
).

Since
\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}=(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})^{2}\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\{f_{1}\}_{q}
and
\left\langle{\varphi_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}=\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}
, by periodicity of
\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle
and (
9.33
), (
9.34
), (
9.37
),

\displaystyle\|\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim

\displaystyle q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{2}\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{2}\qquad\text{(applying \eqref{eq:Fourier MVT} twice)};

(9.40)

\displaystyle\|\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim

\displaystyle q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\qquad\text{(applying \eqref{eq:Fourier MVT} once)};

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}\|=

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}\|+\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|\dot{f}_{1}\|+\|\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}\|=

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}\|+\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|+\|\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;

Using the first line of (
9.19
), we have
\widetilde{g}_{1}=(1+\varphi_{1})^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}
.
By (
9.38
) and (
9.40
),

\|\widetilde{g}_{1}\|\lesssim\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}\|\lesssim\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|.

We may now estimate the last two terms of (
9.24
).
Using Lemma
C.2
with
\delta=\omega-p/q
and the preceding bounds (
9.36
), (
9.37
) on
f_{1}
as well as the first line of (
9.40
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{g}_{1}-\Big(\omega-\frac{p}{q}\Big)\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}\right\|\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}-(\omega-\frac{p}{q})\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}\right\|+\left\|\frac{\{\varphi_{1}^{+}\}_{q}-(1+\widetilde{g}_{1})\left\{{\varphi_{1}}\right\}_{q}}{1+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{2}\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|+q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{2}\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{2}.

We have established the first inequality of (
9.31
).
To approximate
\widetilde{g}_{2}
, it is necessary to bound the second term of (
9.27
), which in turn requires bounds on
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
as well as
\varphi_{2}
.
To this end, let us first use definition (
9.22
) of
\psi_{1}
, inequalities (
9.33
), (
9.34
), (
9.37
) and assumption (
9.30
) to obtain

\|\psi_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|\dot{f}_{1}\|+q\|f_{1}\|\lesssim q;\qquad\|\dot{\psi}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|+q\|\dot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim q

(9.41)

and

\|\ddot{\psi}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|+q\|\ddot{f}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}.

(9.42)

Similarly, by definition (
9.22
) and (
9.39
), (
9.41
),

\|\psi_{2}\|\lesssim q^{2}\Big(\|f_{2}\|+\|\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}\|+\|(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\|+\|(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}\|+\|(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}\|\Big)\lesssim q^{3}.

By a direct computation, we find that
\dot{\psi}_{2}
is given by

-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{-2}\left\{{\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Big(\dot{f}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\ddot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\ddot{\psi}_{1}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\ddot{f}_{1}+(1+f_{1})\ddot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}\dot{f}_{1}}\right\}_{q}.

Thus, by (
9.39
) and (
9.42
),

\displaystyle\|\dot{\psi}_{2}\|\lesssim

\displaystyle q^{2}\Big(\|\dot{f}_{2}\|+\|\ddot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}\|+\|(1+f_{1})\ddot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\|+\|\dot{f}_{1}\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\|+\|(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\ddot{f}_{1}\|+\|(1+f_{1})\ddot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}\|+\|\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}\dot{f}_{1}\|\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle q^{4}.

Then, from the definition (
9.18
) of
\varphi_{2}
, a similar estimate gives

\|\varphi_{2}\|=\bigg\|f_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\psi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}\bigg\|\lesssim q^{3}.

It now follows from (
9.38
) the second line of (
9.19
) that

\|\widetilde{g}_{2}\|\lesssim\bigg\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{2}-\frac{1}{2}(1+\varphi_{1}^{+})\dot{f}_{1}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\varphi}_{1}^{+}-\big((\varphi_{2}-\psi_{1}\dot{\varphi}_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}+\psi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}\big)\bigg\|\lesssim q^{3}.

To recapitulate, we have obtained

\begin{gathered}\|\psi_{1}\|,\|\dot{\psi}_{1}\|\lesssim q;\quad\|\ddot{\psi}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2};\quad\|\psi_{2}\|\lesssim q^{3};\quad\|\dot{\psi}_{2}\|\lesssim q^{4};\quad\|\varphi_{1}\|<\frac{1}{18};\quad\|\dot{\varphi}_{1}\|\lesssim 1;\\
\|\ddot{\varphi}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2};\quad\|\varphi_{2}\|\lesssim q^{3};\quad\|\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|^{2};\quad\|\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;\\
\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}\|\lesssim\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;\quad\|\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;\quad\|\widetilde{g}_{1}\|\lesssim\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|;\quad\|\widetilde{g}_{2}\|\lesssim q^{3}.\end{gathered}

(9.43)

Now we are ready to approximate
\widetilde{g}_{2}
by estimating the last term of (
9.27
).
Since
\|(1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q})^{-2}\|\lesssim 1
, we may focus on the terms within the square bracket of (
9.27
).
Since
(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{2})\psi_{2}=(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q})(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}+\mathcal{D}_{p/q})\psi_{2}
, we may use (
9.34
) together with the inequalities summarized in (
9.43
) to bound the first two terms as follows.

\displaystyle\left\|(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{2}-\mathcal{D}_{p/q}^{2})\psi_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\left\langle{f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}}\right\rangle_{q}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|\bigg(\|\dot{\psi}_{2}\|+\left\|\left\langle{\dot{f}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\ddot{f}_{1}\psi_{1}^{+}+(1+f_{1})\ddot{\psi}_{1}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\dot{\psi}_{1}^{+}}\right\rangle_{q}\right\|\bigg)\lesssim q^{4}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|.

The rest of the terms can be controlled by a routine application of (
9.43
):

\displaystyle\bigg\|\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\{\dot{\varphi}_{1}\}_{q}-\frac{1}{2}\{\varphi_{1}\}_{q}\dot{f}_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}\bigg\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|

\displaystyle\bigg\|\left\langle{(1+f_{1})\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{f}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\varphi_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}\bigg\|\lesssim q^{2}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|

\displaystyle\|(2+\left\langle{f_{1}}\right\rangle_{q}+\varphi_{1})\{\varphi_{1}\}\widetilde{g}_{2}+(\varphi_{2}-\psi_{1}\dot{\varphi}_{1})\widetilde{g}_{1}+\psi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\dot{\varphi}_{1}\|\lesssim q^{3}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|.

We have shown

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{g}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\langle\dot{f}_{1}\rangle_{q}}{1+\langle f_{1}\rangle_{q}}\right\|\lesssim q^{4}\left|{\omega-\frac{p}{q}}\right|

and completed the proof of (
9.31
).
Finally, we turn to the estimate (
9.32
) of
\widetilde{s}_{2}
.
By the second line of (
9.28
) and the bounds on
